WOMEN'S CHAT. ' ITheir Majesties the King and Queen both attended christenings at the Chapel Royal St. James's before Igoing to Sandringham for Christmas. The King VMS sponsor to the infant son of Viscount and Viscountess Castlereagh, and the Queen was godmother to the little son of the Duke and Duchess of Manchester. The Duchess is the daughter of M. Eugene Zimmerman, of Cincinnati, and is therefore one of the many American women in society. Her marriage with the duke created some sensation as it only became known after the event, when it was discovered to have quietly taken place in a London church, in the presence of four or five intimate friends. The King and Queen always delight to follow the old fashioned custom of spending Christmas in the country. Last year their Majesties were kept in London by the Queen's indisposition,but with very few exceptions they have always spent Christmas at Sandringham,sarrounded by their family. There have always been some Royal little folks present for whom Christmas is the one occasion of the year, and the old Yuletide customs are always fully observed at Sandringham. Such also is the case at most of the country seats of the landed aristocracy. Amongst the middle classes there has perhaps been some tendency in recent years to break away from the old fixed idea that on Christmas Day one must either entertain,or be a guest at a family party,hut great houses are generally full to overflowing at Christ- mas time. This year many people have gone to India for the Durbar, and for this reason there are no house parties at Blenheim or Welbeck, but there are Christmas gatherings at Chatsworth, Trentha miloodwood,Dalkeith, ittinghame, and other houses of the great, too numerous to mention. Among the many superstitions connected with New Year's eve is the very uncomplimentary one to our sex that under no circumstances must a woman be the first to cross the threshold after 12 o'clock, for shealways brings bad luck. This belief is entertained in many Icountry places, and also with regard to the first foot the question whether it is a fair or dark person is "thought to bring good or bad luck: A common practice among cottagers is for the housewife, in raking out the fire, to spread the ashes on the floor, in expectation of finding a footprint in the morning. If it points towards the door then someone is going to die, but if it points inwards, then there will be an addition to the family in the course of the year. Again on New Year's morning, the housewife sweeps the floor from the door to the hearth, lest in doing it the other way about,all the good luck of the family is swept out of the house till next New Year's Day. One of the prettiest stories of a courtship is that con- tainedin Oliver Wendell Holmes' popular book "The Autocrat of the Breakfast Table" of which anew edition has just been published—(J. M. Dent & Co.) The autocrat had a friend, a schoolmistress with whom he had been taking some pleasant walks, but no words of love had been spoken until one day when Holmes had taken a passage from the United States to England, with the condition that he was to be released if circum- stances detained him. "I felt very weak indeed (though of a tolerably robust habit) as we came opposite the head of this path on that morning. I think I tried to speak twice without making myself distinctly audible. At last I got out the question 'Will you take the long path with me?' 'Certainly,' said the schoolmistress, with much pleasure.' Think,' I said, before you answer, if you take the long path with me now, I shall interpret it that we are to part no more,' 'Tile school- mistress stepped back with a sudden movement as if an arrow had struck her.' One of the long granite blocks used as seats was hard by, the one you may still see, close by the gingko tree. 'Pray sit down,' said I. 'No, no," she answered softly, 'I will take the long path with yon." Madame Calve, the great French singeris one of those people who are unhappy because they have gained all that they wanted. It is much more frequently the case that people are miserable because they are not success- ful, but Madame Calve is joyless because there is nothing left for her to strive after. She is sick of success tired of applause, weary of money making, and says that if she could live her life again, she would seek the happiness of an uneventful bourgeois existence. Possibly Madame Patti mayhavesometimes experienced a fellow feeling when nothing but a repetition of former triumphs was left to her. She too must have wearied of making money, for Madame Patti often re- ceived as much as £70,000 in a single year, and alto- gether she must have made a good deal more than a million pounds with her wonderful voice. For afternoon wear, white tulle or chiffon blouses, inlet with lace, much tucked, and mounted on white silk are almost a livery, and very dainty and dressy they look, when coats and furs are opened and thrown back. The wise woman will however see to it that a warm flannel slip, high to the throat is worn under such ethereal garb, otherwise the consequences may be unpleaasant in the extreme. Several modistes are trimming these white tulle or chiffon blouses with nar- row bands of ermine, the slightly pouched front being' lightly sprinkled with the tiny tails. On white' black or grey, this particular form of trimming is a distant success, but when bright colours have to be taken into consideration, the combination is an undesirable one. Dressing gowns, and bed wraps, are more dainty than ever before and very moderate in price. The prettiest are built of zanana, a sort silky fabric, not unlike crepe-de-chine, very becoming and very warm. In 'cut they resemble the Japanese Kimons, and a favorite form of trimming is Japanese crape, laid on in flat bands. There is, it must be confessed, very little wear in this beautiful material, but it is not expensive, and the bands can easily be renewed. A favorite, and very acceptable Christmas present has been the lace collar, or fichu, one or other being practically a necessity on every dress. All partake of the early Victorian character, the tippet fichu narrow- ing to vanishing point at the waist, the shawl-collar falling deeply over the shoulders. Irish lace is still enjoying a wide vogue, indeed there is quite a rage for everything Irish. Poplin,' especially in the pale shades is being extensively employed, by leading dressmakers, the rich, soft stuff lending itself well to the desired long lines, and graceful draperies. A sash,and sundry touches of crêpe-de-chine, are usually added to such gowns. The fuchsia is, in the trimming world, the flower of the moment. Appliques in soft silk, are to be seen on many of the newest cloth gowns, and the drooping blooms in chenille, or in lace, are finding their way on to many a dainty ball gown. By the way, a few skirts for dancing have appeared lately, which only just sweep the ground. The pleasure of dancing would be heighened considerably for both men and girls, if the mode were to become a general one, but alas,, the majority of fashionable dressmakers still refuse to build I the trainless ball skirt.

MADGE.

THE FREE CIiURCHEX

EDUCATION:

Although the "Educational " measure of the Government has now passed into law, the controversy is by no means done with. A very largely' attended meeting of the Educa- tion and Legal Committees of the National Free Church Council has been held in London, and it was decided that the National Council will continue to take the meet vigorous action in every possible way. A strong resolution was passed reiterating the objections of the Free Churches to the Bill, emphatically declining to accept the measure as a settlement, and announcing the intention of the Council to continue to work to replace it on the Statute Book by an Act on national lines, and really unifying education. Arrangements are being made for the Legal Committee of the National Camcil to meet on the first Thursday in each I,lonth to consider legal questions raised by the local councils. Tlw c unicils are being invited to submit tluir dii:iculties to this Committee. Representatives from the National Council are also meeting. in special consultaLion with fore- most educational experts. it. is suggested that almost immediately the Bill Lecomes law com- munications will lie made to every'niinister and church throuchout the country. It is expected that the National Council meetings at Brighton will be made the nccasion for launching an effective policy.

===== 0002601_19021226_art0025.txt | included=true | included =====
A •PItESSING DUTY.

Meanwhile it bAhoves all Free Churchmen, in the exercise of their rights as citizeum, to care- fully watch the local preparations tor the carry- ing out of the measure, ant/ ut.e :vcry effort to secure the preparatimi end presentation of schemes which Khali be as eiicient educationally, and as little injurious to the interests of National Education, and of the Free Churches, as they can possibly be made.

===== 0002601_19021226_art0026.txt | included=true | included =====
PASSIVE RESISTANCE. .

It is announced that a special committee to deal with the policy of passive resistance has now been organised. It consists of the following, among others: Dr. Clifford, Dr. Townsend, Rev. Thomas Card, Rev. Dr. Aked, Rev. James Travis, Dr. Massie, Rev. T. Hirst Hollowell, Dr. Rendal Harris, Mr. Alfred Illing worth, Mr. George White, M.P., Principal T. A. Mitchell, Mr. Robert Bird, and Rev. R. T. Campbell, M.A.

===== 0002601_19021226_art0027.txt | included=true | included =====
VISITING ENGLAND.

Two American evangelists are shortly visiting England, whose coming should create something of a stir—the Retr. H. A. Torrey, D.D., Principal of the Moody Bible Institute, Chicago, and Mr. Chas. Alexander, a young choirmaster and singer of great ability. Dr. Torrey and Mr. Alexander have been conducting a series of missions in Australia, and the results have been in many cases remarkable. A simultaneous Mission was held in Melbourne, at the close of which no less than eight thousand persons professed conversion. Sydney, Tasmania, and New Zealand and other places have been visited with similar results. Dr. Torrey intends to tour the world, and has already held revival meetings in China and Japan. A full account of the Australian meetings appears in the Free Church Chronicle for January.

===== 0002601_19021226_art0028.txt | included=true | included =====
HAVE WE TOO MANY MEETINGS?

An interesting point is touched on in an interview with the Rev. J. B. Meharry, Di)., the well-known minister of Crouch-hill, North London, in the Free Church Chrankle. Dr. llyieharry asks, "Have we not too many meet- ings, where the presence of the minister, whether for ornament or use, is supposed to be a necessity P Would many of our young people not be better educated for God and the work, if Otey would take a night off from attendance at meetings, and devote it to prayer, serious reading of a classic, whether of religious or pro- fane literature, needed rest, and thinking? My opinion is, if that practice were more generally adopted there would be a healthier tone in the religious life of our young people, their thoughts would be more definite and masculine, and the roots of their faith less slender."

===== 0002601_19021226_art0029.txt | included=true | included =====
EDUCATIONAL LEGAL HANDBOOK.

The National Council of the Evangelical Free Churches is about to publish immediately an Education Legal Handbook. It will contain a complete text of the Government Education Act, with full explanatory notes, showing how the working of it will affect the Free Churches. All necessary legal information will be given. It is being prepared by Mr. E. C. Rawlings, author of "The Free Churchman's Legal Hand- book." The volume will also contain an his- torical sketch of Education in England and Wales from the Nonconformist standpoint, written by Rev. W. J. Townsend, D.D., Presi- dent of the National Council.

===== 0002601_19021226_art0030.txt | included=true | included =====
TUE CITY TEDIIsLE.

The deacons of the City Temple have re- quested the Rev. R. J. Campbell, M.A., of Brighton, to assume permanent responsibility for the Thursday noonday services. The deacons add that this arrangement refers to Thursdays only, and has no relation to filling the vacant pastorate. Many men have been suggested as successors to Dr. Parker, but, of course, nothing definite has yet been done in the matter of choosing a minister. The City Temple occupies a unique position in English Nonconformity, and Dr. Parker's equally unique presence will he missed for some time to come; his place cannot be adequately filled by any other.

TEE RELIGIOUS CENSUS-TAKING.

A good deal of interest is being taken in the project of the Daily News to ascertain the at- tendances at the churches of the metropolis. Already the returns of Battersea, Kensington, Harlipstead, and Paddington have been pub- lished, and the work continues Sunday by Sun- day. At present little more is established than that the different districts have striking varia- tions, and that we cannot estimate for one borough the state of things in another, or in London as a whole. When the results are finally tabulated some interesting facts should be established as to the comparisors of atten- dances between the Free and the Established Churches, the proportion of attendances in the various districts—sill:my-ban, East-end, West- end, &c., and the composition of the congrega- tions.

===== 0002601_19021226_art0031.txt | included=true | included =====
A REMARKABLE BOOK.

So great an interest was taken in the auto- biography of Gipsy Smith. the well-known missioner, and so great was the demand, that the publishers have issued a chap edition. six thousand copies of this were so!d within a few days of publication, and the demand continues to be phenomenally great. Tho price of the new edition is 2s. C. net.

===== 0002601_19021226_art0032.txt | included=true | included =====
METUODIST UNION.

A few days ago a gathering of representative Methodists from three of the lesser denomina- tions met in London to discuss questions in relation to Methodist Union. The United Methodist Free Church, the Methodist New Connexion, and the Bible Christians had 'each appointed 12 representatives to confer, but without any power of initiative action. The result of their deliberations will be reported to the respective conferences next June or July. It is to be hoped that good results will follow the holding of this informal conference.

A startling discovery was made at Dulwich on Mon- day afternoon. A caretaker of Underhill-road, Dulwich, "Wag walking over a piece of waste ground whenhe came -upon a soap box, which he found to contain the dead body of a male child between three and four days old. It had been a very healthy child, and was attired in clothing of good quality. Every attention, medicaland otherwise, had apparently been paid at the time of birth.

HOCKEY.

STOCKTON HEATH V. SALE.—Played at Stockton Heath on Saturday, ion a sticky ground. A well con- tested game ended n a win for Sale. Final :—Sale, two goals; Stockton Heath, one.

• SALE "A" V. STOCKTON HEATH "A."—At Sale on Saturday. Stockton Heath had only ten men, playing without a goalkeeper, which rather handicapped the Sale forwards, who were frequently off-side. Sale had a little the best of a vigorous game, and Brown scored once in the first half. Brown scored a very clever goal in the second ,half, the home team winning by two goals to nil. The threequarters and centre half for Stockton Heath played a sound game, while Sale were best repre- sented by Brown and B. Thornber at forward.

LATCH FORD V. SALE "B."—These teams met at Sale on Saturday in the return match. The previous en- counter resulted in a win for Latchford, by five to one, as this was the only loss Sale have suffered this season. Saturday's game was keenly anticipated, the home team being anxious to retrieve themselves. The game was fast and exciting. Cliffe, for the visitors, secured the ball in the circle, and with a good shot notched the first point. The game was now even more vigorous, the home team making desperate attempts to equalize, but the visitors' defence was always sound. The visitors again made another attack on the home goal, and Cliff() was successful with a second shot. Nothing further was scored and an exceedingly pleasant and interesting game resulted in a win for Latchford by two goals to nil. This is rather creditable to the visit- ors as thty are the only team who have brought about the downfall of Sale for three seasons.

' There was no shadow of danger that the imita- tion would be detected, Mr. Gerald knew that very well. He hid calculated the possibilities, and argued to himself, very reasonably, that paste would do just as well to lie year after year unused in his mother's bureau. i She would be no loser from the substitution, • and he would be immeasurably the gainer. He would make Miss Irma's eyes sparkle grate- fully, and he would get some extra rocket-money himself, and really his action harmed no one. Before I had finished 'breakfast I had almost persuaded myself that he had • committed quite a meritorious action. .

But I hoped they would get that miniature painted quickly. I feared my mistress would not agree with me as to the merit of Mr. Gerald's action, should any untoward accident reveal it. It would kill her more quickly than physical torture.

I knew now why Mr. Gerald's fun and gaiety were not so spontaneous as usual. "A mind ill at .ase." does not conduce to high spirits. He must his punishment for the present. at any rate. I could not tell him I knew, that would be worse still. His suffering was the inevitable and inexorable law of cause and effect. set, after all, as it a soul sin, or only, a physical sin? I could not make u my mind. , It was more than a week after my visit to town before I saw Miss Irma. She had been away from home for two or three days with her mother, staying with Mr. Densmore. His horse was to run in a county steeplechase, and lie was to ride the horse himself; and a large house-party had been invited for the occasion and various festivities planned. But ten days later caine the peremptory little tap that I loved to hear at the studio door. It made my heart beat glite painfully fast, for in the top drawer of my davenport beside the window lay a morocco ease lined with pale-green velvet. • Should I give it to her to-day, or wait till next week, which would be Christmas week, or for the hunt ball just after Christmas? I had no time to decide this knotty question, for Miss Irma rushed in after her usual impulsive fashion, and almost springing on to my lap, cast both arms about my neck and kissed me till .I laughingly cried for a truc3.

She was in her riding-habit and splashed from neck to hem with mud, which she was diligently rubbing off on my afternoon gown, which boasted ribbon trimmings of a delicate fawn colour. "Yon are spoiling my ribbons, Irma," I cried, trying to push her off, but she only held me more tightly. "You can buy a manufacturer's stock of fresh ones, you terribly rich person," she answered, and kissed me again. "There! that for punishment. hope your face is quite sore, you darling Rachel. I dream of my lovely cloak by night, and sit in it by day before the glass. I must tell Lady Isabella all about it " : and with a final hug she extPhanged my lap for a footstool beside Lady Isabella, who was sitting up to-day painting, and, for her, com- paratively well. But, before her rhapsodies were exhausted, to my relief the door opened without any warning knock, and Sir George Dudley came in. He, too, was in riding dress, a cute and judioious mixture of the cleric and the sportsman, and ,almost as badly bespattered with mud as Irma. "0 Sir George," exclaimed Mies Irma, springing up. "Didn't we have a glorious run? . Such lovely soft muddy lines." "So it, seems," said Lady Isabella, leaning back Fe,,ile up at Sir George. "Did you enjoy it, dear?" .

. . "It was too short; that was the great pity," replied Sir George. "But, my dear, Densmore is downstairs; he rode home with us. Are you well enough for me to bring him here for some tea? I think you would like him." Certaitnly, dear. I am better than usual to- day. Rachel will , order tea at once. Oh, here' come the lamps." I • Thomas entered as she spoke with a shaded lamp in either hand. I drew she curtains and threw more wood on the fire which lenped into vivid flame just in tune to illuminate Mr. Densmore as he entered with Sir Gorge. I had heard so much of Mr. Densmore and his large house, his large business, and his large income, that I was quite thrown off my bearings to find he was personally a small man, slightly inclined to be bulky, clean shaven, and well groomed. He was in pink, and apologised in perfect style to Lady Isabella for his bespattered hunting costume. His, voice was the pleasant voice of a gentleman by nature, his only peculiarity that he pronounced words like "dance," and "grass," and "glass," with the narrow a. "Well, Rachel, you don't admire it," whispered Irma, as she came.to my tea-table and tipped half the jug of cream into her own cup. And she turned her head towards me so that I noticed what had not before attracted my attention —a gold arrow with tiny diamond feathers stuck through her hair, which was tightly coiled for hunting. "What a risk to wear it hunting. You might so easily lose it." • "He only gave- it to me this morning. I could not resist wearing it. Isn't it delicious ?" "Yes, lovely; but—" "But what ? " - -- .

Nothing. There .is Mr. Gerald just come in. perhaps he has brought you something." Mr. Gerald had been to town that day, and as I spoke he was laying several parcels on the little table beside his mother preparatory to shaking hands with Mr. Densmore.

"Give me some tea for him, Rachel; quick, please, there's a dear ?" "For who?" I asked. i "For Gerald. I want to see those parcels, they look most interesting:".

"They are," I replied. "Then I will put him an extra lump of sugar," she said. "Ile can't bear sugar," I replied. She made a little face of disgust and carried the tea to him. He knew very well that she had an eye to the parcels lying there, and did not pretend to thank her; but he was pleased that Mr. Denimore should .see her waiting upon him. As she turned he caught sight of the glittering arrow in her fair hair, and he called to. iter : "Do you want to see my parcels, Irma ? " "Not if they are diamond pins and waistcoat buttons," she said. "But they are tiaras and brooches and butter- flies," he replied. "0 Gerald! For me? Did you win your wager?" "Yes, I did, and I have had my mother's iiamonds re-set." He was busy with one of the smaller parcels, unwrapping it whilst Lady Isabella was indifferently disrobing another as she talked to Mr. Densmore. From my tea-table I watched them, both with an interest so intense that it Made me feel quite sick.;

If Mr. Gerald did but know that I shared his secret!

Miss Irma's cry of delight when the open case revealed the tremulous jewelled butterfly drew everyonee attention to the gift; and Gerald's exultation that Mr. Densmore should chance to be present, would, I knew, more than compensate him for any stabs his conscience might give him. Whatever happened in the future he had had his moment—nothing could rob him of that. Mr. Densmore bent closely over•the trinket as it lay in Irma's pink-cushioned palm, and scurtinised it keenly.. "It is a beauty," he said frankly, as he raised his head. "You are a good judge of - stones. Mr. Du'elley.: I consider myself almost an expert in, diamonds; and those, although small, are first class." "Then tell me what you think of mine, Mt. Denimore," said Lady Isabella, opening the case she held in her hand and displaying the tiara I had seen when in town. "They are beauties," 116 said heartily. "I can see that from here. May I look at them more closely ?" '9..re took the case in his hand, and bent down to ate small reading lamp that stood on Lady Isabella's table. I seemed to see Mr. Gerald growing white and uneasy. Was it possible there could be any danger that Mr. Densmore would detect the imitation? I was absolutely numb with anxiety. He took a small magnifying-glass from his waist- coat-pocket and .19,oked at theta through that.

*You seem to find my stones interesting, Mr. Densmore," said Lady Isabella. "What do you think of them?"

He hesitated a moment, then he said frankly: "To tell you the truth, Lady Isabella, they puzzle me. They are either vamaore stOnes or 'a . wonderfully good imitation. , "They are not imitation," said Lady Isabella : laughing. "May I look at them by the other lamp ? " said Mr. Densmore. "It is a stronger light.' He came towards my table as he spoke. What was to be done? Without an instant's hesitation I leant forward and turned out the lamp, and turned it so rapidly that I felt pretty sure the wick must have dropped into the oil, so that it could not be relighted in a moment. It would give time. Everyone laughed. They all thought it was an accident. I apologised and began hastily to try and relight the lamp, and to explain that the wick had vanished' and in the midst of my explana- tions I was thankful to hear Mr. Densmore bidding Lady Isabella good-bye, and asking if he might be allowed to inspect the diamonds by daylight some time.

The danger was over for the moment, bat Mr. Gerald looked shaken. Only he and I knew that we were all standing on a volcano, and I dared not let him know that help was at hand. How long would those Bond-street people be in getting that miniature finished? I must write about it at once. __ CHAPTER VII. -

"That was a curious mistake for the careful hand of Mistress Rachel to make," said the voice of Mr. Gerald close beside me, as the door closed on his father and Mr. Densmore.

"How did you• manage it, Rachel ? " asked Irma, leaning over the table, and peering down the chimney of the lamp. "An unseen power impelled me inversely," I replied, gathering the cups together for Thomas to carry away. "An evil spirit, Rachel ? " said Irma, pretending to look scared.

"I think it was a beneficent one," I said drily. I don't believe in accident. Do you want any more tea, Mr. Gerald?"

"No, Rachel, thank you. I have brought you a present." "I hope it is not diam oads," I exclaimed. I WAS so surprised at his bringing me anything that I said it before I had time to think; but Mr. Gerald did not appear to consider it a curious remark. Knowing how little I prized jewellery of any find, he would not detect any sarcasm in my exclamation, but accept it as bond-fide. "It is from Mr. Bathurst rather than from myself," he replied. "I not bim At the station, and he ptve me this for you, and told me to give you besides his warm congratqlstions on your brother's article, which, he saysAis scholarly and keenly intelligent; those were his yery words: Scholarly, and keenly intelligent.' " "My brother's article!" I sa; i, staring blankly at him. "What do you mean, Mr. Gerald?" "Look here, and you will see, "he answered. And, as Thomas returned with the re-lighted lamp, Mr. Gerald took up the copy of the Bi-monthly, which he had laid on the table, and ran his finger down the table of contents. ' "See," lie said.

I looked where he had pointed, and read: "The Result of Compulsory Education on the Masses," by Paul Howard. Paul an author! His name in large type in one of our leading magazines! His remarks read care- fully by clergymen like Mr. Bathurst! How my thoughts went back to the cottage home where, years ago, mother would say to Paul: "You'll surely go blind, lad, if you pore over those books so much," and father would say, kindly : "Let him enjoy himself, mother, the learning one gets for love of it is the Only learning worth having." There was no compulsory education when Paul was a little lad. People of our class learnt to read, write, and cypher, and then they were left nobody tried to force the cream to the top in those days— it rose by itself; and I think it was better and rivber.

I turned,the leaves of tie magazine' till I found the article, and began to read. Mr. Gerald stood L....eside me, and Irma leant over my shoulder ; we all read it together so. It was not king, barely three pages of the magazine ; but it was like Paul—strong, earnest, concentrated ; each word was to the a point, a blow s,raight from the shoulder, and not a word too much. I could hare laid my face down on the leaves of the magazine and enjoyed a little weep, my heart was so full of joy and pride ; but I was not alone, and I swallowed my emotion and looked up at Irma fot sympathy. Her eyes were bright and her cheeks flushed. but she only exclaimed hastily : "I'm not clever, Rachel ; 1 don't know whether it is scholarly, and keenly intelligent, ' but the spelling and the grammar are all right, ain't they, Gerald ? Oh, isn't my diamond butterfly lovely ?" and she dashed away across the room, and Mr. Gerald followed her, to admire all the trinkets once more, whilst I read Paul's article through a second time.

Presently Irma came back to me whilst Mr. Gerald put away his mother's diamonds. "Yon look as proud as Mrs. Brown with her new baby,' she said, sitting down ori the floor in front of me. "Where is Paul, Rachel ? Why doesn't he come to see you ?" "I don't know," I said. "He has reasons, probably." "I wish he would come back and let everything be nice and condonable, as it used to bet"

"I e will never do tha' t," I said proudly; "he is more likely to be Prime Minister." What a hateful thing money is! I had no pride in the days when we were poor. Now, I actually : resented my sweet Miss Irma's remarks. But she only made a little grimace, and said: "Paul has spoilt me ever since the day he carried me home from church through the snowstorm, when I was a wee dot and he was a great strong boy, and I miss him, Rachel. I don't want him to write ,1 articles or be a Prime Minister." "You are a selfish little creature," I said. "See, the carriage is waiting to take you home ; run, and leave ins to read Paul's article over again." _ _ _

I had forgotten all about the diamond necklace lyin<, in the drawer of my davenport. I had in- tended to give it rh Irma that evening, but the fear that Mr. Densmore was going to detect the paste, and Paul's article together, had driven other matters out of my head. Never mind, the necklace would keep; and Miss Irma had had enough presents for the moment. If she got it in time for the hunt ball, it would do very well. My thoughts went back to Paul and to Mr. Densmore, and tossed about between the two as I knitted, until my attention was attracted by Mr. Densmore's name. "Mr. Densmore seemed somewhat doubtful about my diamonds," Lady Isabella was saying to Mr. Gerald. "Do you think he knows anything about them ? "

"I don't suppose he knows more than other amateurs," said Gerald lightly. "I heard him ask your father if he might examine them again with his lens." "I wish, mother, you would let Irma have them for the hunt ball," said Gerald, reeking decidedly anxious.

"I don't think they are suitable for a clergyman's daughter, Gerald; and such a young girl. And I don t think it fair to her or to me to lend such valuable jewels. If she ever becomes your wife," added Lady Isabella, after a moment's hesitation, "the diamonds will be her own to wear when she will."

Poor Mr. Gerald! I chanced to be looking at him when his mother said this. His face was a study. Evidently he had not looked a step beyond the present moment. It had never occurred to him that in the natural course of events the diamonds would one day go to Irma, if he won her for his wife. He had argued that paste would do quite well to lie unused in his mother's bureau. He had not remembered that his wife would have to wear the paste! And there was the constant risk that Mr. Densmore—or someone else—would detect the imitation. How short-sighted men are! I hoped more than ever that they would be quick in getting that miniature finished. I had a fellow-feeling for Mr. Gerald—Miss Irma's fascination was over mil quite as strongly as it was over him. And, after all, did his action harm anyone? Jewels and money are such temporary things. But this was dangerous reasoning; I had better give it up, and read Paul's clever article once. more. And as I read it, I wondered if Paul were in London? - (To be continued.)

CHRISTMAS EATING---AND AFTER.

"What a blessing is a good digestion," exclaimed Anthony Trollope, and thousands since have repeated his words. About Christmas time however, the other side of the subject which may be expressed in the ex- clamation, "What a curse is a bad digestion," is most commonly before one's mind. A very distinguished physician has said, "I think the cause of most of the maladies of the human race lies in their stuffing their digestive organs to excess." This expresses in very blunt fashion the truth borne home at Xmastide. Indigestion, the result of Xmas eating and drinking, is at this period painfully rife. As a handy, safe and reliable cure, not only for indigestion but for heartburn, sour stomach, headache, and other symptoms of Xmas indiscretions in diet, Chas. Forde's bile beans are unequalled. They are not sugar coated, and may be carried loose in the waistcoat or dress pocket. One or two taken after a heavy meal will prevent any unpleasant conse- quences. A dose taken at night will be sufficient to prevent any "nasty taste in the mouth," dry tongue, or other unpleasant morning symptoms. Bile Beans are obtainable from all chemists at one and three-halfpence or two and nine per box, or post free from the Bile Bean Manufacturing Co., Red Cross-street, London, E.C., upon receipt of prices- Purely vegetable in com- position bile beans are now recognised as the greatest specific for all liver and stomach disorders, and are pre- scribed by many eminent medical men.

WARRINGTON AND DISTRICT AGRICULTURAL SOCIETY. Handsome Donation by Sir Gilbert Greenall.

, Sir GILB ER,T GREENALL Bart. presided at the annual meeting of the Warrington and District Agricultural Society, held at the Masonic Rooms, Bold-street, War- rington' on Monday afternoon. Amongst those present were: Mr. John White, Mr. Cohn Maclver (hon secre- tary), Mr. Joseph Knowles (hon treasurer), Mr. H. Beresford Melville (secretary), Mr. H. F. Greenough, Mr. Whitlow, Mr. A. Broome, Mr. S. Gould (Lymm), and others.

===== 0002601_19021226_art0046.txt | included=true | included =====
STATEMENT OF ACCOUNTS.

The statement of accounts showed that the receipts amounted to £551 ss. and the payments £836 7s. 10d. leaving a deficit on the year's working of £285 2s. 10d. Sir Gilbert Greensll contributed a special donation of £3OO, which together with a balance in hand of £52 9s. 9d. in 1901, enables the society to wipe out last year's deficit and commence the forthcoming year with a balance in hand of £67 6s. 11d. . _

The accounts were passed on the proposition of Mr. A. BROOME.

ANNUAL REPORT.

The report read as follows i—The 13th annual show of the Warrington and District Agricultural. Society was held by the kind invitation of the President, Sir Gilbert Greenall, Bart., at Walton Park on Thursday, August • 7th. From an exhibition point ofiview it was if any- thing another advanceupon previous years. The classes were well filled and an expression of opinion obtained from all the judges as to the excellence of the exhibits , throughout was most gratifying to the committee and ' officers of the society for the\ great trouble and perse- verance they had taken in the formation of the society • and the creation of a show second to none in the , local shows il of the country. The committee, however, regret to say that they are not receiv- , ing that gen oral support they can conscienti- ously ask for from the immediate neighbourhood. ' The society does not yet pay its way, the President ! having once more to supplement his support by an addi- tional large donation to meet the deficiency in the year's workings. The expenses seem to increase as years go on. Additional advertising is required and above all there is the greatest difficulty in obtaining the necessary entry of exhibits although the exhibits are actually ready and the exhibitors prepared to show. It is estim- ated that at least one half of the exhibitors had to be personally approached for their entries and above all I considerable trouble is experienced in the entries being , sent in some days after the lists are supposed to be , closed. The committee are of opinion that a certain day should now be agreed upon and on that day the I entry list should be closed and no entries taken on any consideration whatever. The stibscription list is not increasing as might be expected. If the society is to re- main as a local society, supported by local contributions ! there ought not to be the least difficulty in obtaining the necessary financial support. The question therefore remains has the Warrington Agricultural Society done its duty to the neighbourhood in furthering theinter- ests.of agriculture, and if so why does the neighbour- hood hesitate to offer that most necessary filliprequired 1 to promote the welfare of a society of its kind. It is suggested that a strong appeal has never really been I made. Perhaps not; but we would now submit our ' appeal to the neighbourhood and ask: Shall we progress or close eur society as a local institution? Warrington undoubtedly is a great centremidway between Liverpool and Manchester, Preston and Crewe, and ought to be the pivot round which agriculturists should assemble and to a great extent they do. It is however, fair to say that the Walton Show may not have had a good chance for success on account of the weather which since the show was enlarged has been anything but favourable. This, however, only explains the smallness of the takings at the gate. It is suggested to make some changes in the offer of prizes and to do away with certain produce classes which have not proved either interesting or useful. Pig classes were added this year and they were considered very fair with a promise of improvement another year. Other alterations are sug- gested. and the committee will as soon as possible issue a preliminary notice with fuller particulars. In the meantime we ask for your support financially, and in- tending contributors would considerably lessen the clerical work of the society if they would send in their entries punctually, and correctly give the information asked for on the entry forms. Subscriptions are due on or before May Ist and should so be paid to the Secre- tary, otherwise great additional trouble is caused in having to personally apply _for payment. Mr. WHITLOW proposed the adoption of the report. Mr. S. GOULD seconded, and it was unanimously agreed to. RE-ELECTION OF PRESIDENT.

Mr. H. F. GREENOUGH proposed that Sir Gilbert Greenall, Bart., be re-elected president. He thought the society was very fortunate in having such a gentleman at the head of affairs and one who took such an interest in its welfare.. , Mr. JOSEPH KNowLEs seconded the proposition, and it was unanimously carried. Sir GILBERT GREENA.LI, said he would be very pleased to act as president if it was the wish of the society. (Hear, hear.) THE VICE-PRESIDENT. Sir GILBERT said the next business was the election of a vice-president. It was always their prac- tice to elect a landowner or a gentleman interested in agriculture from Lancashire one year, and Cheshire the next. It was Cheshire's turn this year and he proposed that Mr. J. St. Vigor Fox, be asked to be vice-president for the ensuing year. He was a very large landowner and took a great interest in all matters connected with agriculture. Mr. H. F. GREENOUGH seconded and the proposition was unanimously agreed to. Mr. COLIN IVLaclvEn said he could say on behalf of Mr. Fox that he would accept the position. (Hear, hear.)

===== 0002601_19021226_art0047.txt | included=true | included =====
THE GENERAL COMMITTEE.

The.following were appointed on the committee in additionto the President and Vice-President : Messrs, R. Ainscough,A. Broome. T,Charnock,R. Clark,S, Davies, H. Greenough, J. Gould, S Gonli, E. Gibbon James Hepherd, W. H. Jones, J. Mainwaring, H, Mawdsley, H. Milling pin., G.Norris Midwood,H. G. Roberts, Joseph Shakeshaft,James Tinsienjun.,F. Tinsley,Joseph Tickle, Colonel Walker, M.P., and Mr. John White. Mr. A. BROOME thought more interest would be taken- in the society if the committee were enlarged. The CHAIRMAN: There are already 24 on the com- mittee. That is a very large committee. Mr. BROOME was of opinion it might be increased to 60. Mr. MACIVER said the meeting could not increase the committee without first altering the rules.

The subject then dropped. - NEXT YEAR'S PRIZE LIST.

IMPORTANT ALTERATIONS.

The SECRETARY said the Executive Committee had considered the prize list for next year's show and sug- gested several important alterations. The shire classes would remain practically the same, with the exception of class seven which would be for two-year old colts or geldings, and class ten which woul4 be for geldings foaled in 1899-1900. In the open hunter classes, the prizes would be reduced in class 19 to first prize £lO, second prize £7, and third prize £4. An additional class would be'made and Mr. Stokes had promised to give a first prize of £lO for the best hunter mare or gelding foaled in 1897-8-9 not exceeding 15 hands 2in. in height. In class 21, the fourth prize would be withheld i there were less than ten entries. The CHAIRMAN said it was suggested that there should be classes for polo ponies. He (Sir Gilbert) saw Mr. Midwood at the polo meeting in London, and that gentleman said he would be pleased to give the prizes in one class. (Hear, hear.) He (Sir Gilbert) was pre- pared to give the prizes in the other class. (Hear, hear.) He thought the additional classes would add to the interest of the show. The SECRETARY, in referring to the classes for trades- men's or tenant farmers' turnouts said it was proposed to increase the number of prizes from 3to 4, and the value would be as follows :—lst prize £5, 2nd prize £3, 3rd prize £2, 4th prize £l. In the cattle classes, in place of classes 41 and 42, there would be one class for young bulls. There would be no classes for eggs, dressed poultry and potatoes. The following would be the prize list :—Shires and draught geldings, 2142 ; hunters, £146; hackneys, £66; harness, £34 ; jumpers, £5O; tradesmen's turnouts, £33 ; polo pony classes. £36 ; bulls, £l9 ;cows, £45; pigs, £45 lOs ; cheese,£6 ; butter, £3 10s. The special prizes suggested were :—Champion cup £lO for shires, silver cup £5 for shire foal, silver cup £3 for best shire exhibit, the property of one of Sir Gilbert Greenall's tenants; Mr. Bretherton's special prize of £5 for shire filly, Mr. Charnock's and Mr. Kenworthy's special prizes. The National Live Stock Insurance Company Limited had also for the first time offered prizes amonAting to £5 (first prize ,21, second 21), in the shire brood mare classes, for the best mare whose owner holds a 12 months foaling policy (classes D E and IT of the company's prospectus). The special prizes for cattle were Mr. Dewhurst's cup and Messrs Day .and Sons medicine chest. Medals will also be given by the Shire Horse Society, the Hunters Improve- ment Society, and the Hackney Horse Society.

and the Hackney ME CHAIRMAN THANKED.

Mr. GREENOUGH proposed a vote of thanks to Sir Gilbert Greenall for presiding. Mr. JOSEPH KNOWLES, in seconding, said all honour was due to their president for his services to the society. (Hear, hear). The proposition was carried. Sir GILBERT GREENALL acknowledged the compli ment and the proceedings terminated.

For the Poor ;Children.

The poor children of the town will have their annual Christmas treat in the Parr Hall on January 14th. Donations will be thankfully received by the Mayoress (who is the president), the members of the committee, or the Eon. Treasurer (Mr. Jenkins, solicitor), and Mr. Henry Taylor (Police Court Missionary) (hon. secre- tary). Ladies willing to dress dolls are requested to 'send their names to the Hon, Secretary at 9, Thynne- street, and he will forward the number required.

The Examin,er, Friday, December 26, 1902.

"By Jove!" he exclaimed, "there's that poor devil who never seems to get a job. I'll send him for the cards." Captain IlalHord tapped at the window to attract the man's attention and beckoned him to come to the front door. In the hall the nature of the commission was briefly explained to him. He was given a sovereign and told to bring back a dozen Christmas cards—Miss Halliford wrote down the manufacturers' names for him—and to be as quick as he Gould.

The substitute took the money, touched his forehead respectfully, and went off promising •to execute his commission with punctuality and despatch. "And now, Robert," said Mrs. Halliford when the Parlour-maid, who had been Stitunioned to dab up the small lake which the overthrown 'vase of flowers had deposited On the table-cloth, had skil- fully performed the operation and retired, "perhaps you will tell me why you were so annoyed with that book. I consider it one of the most charming Christmas stories I have ever read." "And I consider •it confounded TI a idea is idiotic. even for a Christmas story, A pre- posterously impossible .family, all heart and no head, invite an apparently homeless old gentleman to share their Christmas junketings. They see him on their doorstep just as they are sitting dom to the Christmas turkey and plum-pudding. They bring him inside and give him a most indigestible meal. They sit him in the easy chair after dinner and give him port and Brazil nuts, things sufE- cient in themselves to bring on a fit- of apoplexy, and then they all begin to bOre him by telling a story in turn."

"-And a very charming way of spending a Chist- mas afternoon." -

" Absurd! But not content with telling stories them- selves they make the old gentleman tell one, and when he has told it the head of the family, who is a. solicitor on the verge of bankruptcy—of course, through a swindling partner—discovers that the old gentleman is the heir to an immense fortune whom he has been endeavouring to fin& out for years. The old gentleman is taken suddenly ill—l don't wonder at it after the port and Brazil nuts—and is put to bed and nursed by the devoted family. He dies a few weeks afterwards, laving made a will leaving his newly-found fortune to the solicitor on the verge of bankruptoy." "And a very proper thing for him to do, as they had been so kind to him when he was homeless and friendless on Christmas Day I"

"Pickles! The moral's idiotic. Be kind to, an old gentleman on Christmas Day, and you will be happy ever afterwards." Mrs. Halliford shook her head. "I'm sorry to hear you sneer at Christmas sentiment, Robert. I think it is quite right that at the season of peace and good-will we should try to make everybody happy, and that we should be especially, kind to irer, "

"Why on Chriitmas Day? Why not every day? What is there in the i fact of the date n the calendar being the 25:11 of December to make a solicitor on the verge of bankruptcy ask a stranger to come in and have turkey and plum-pudding, and drink port and eat Brazil nuts? "People always did that sort of thing in Christ- rasa stories wheu I was a girl."

"Then you really believe that to fill up this dining-table with outcasts on Christmas Day is a suitable thing for the head of a household to. do ? "

"Yes, I do—most decidedly I do!" exclaimed Mrs. Halliford.

"And you, Lottie--what do you say? "0 papa! of course It's right! I think at Christ- mas time our hearts ought to relent, and we ought to try and make everybody happy." ' She was thinkirg of Dick, who had been for- bidden the house because he was in love with her and wanted to marry her and live in a cottage. The Captain looked from his wife to his daughtcr —from his daughter to his wife. Then he sat down in his chair and thought things out. He was wondering how far they would like to see the beautiful sentiments of the Christmas stories put into practice under their own roof. A ring at the bell interrupted his meditations, and presently the servant entered to say the mes- senger had brought the Christmas cards. "Send him in," said the Captain; and imme- diately afterwards old Joe's substitute stood remectfully just inside the dining-room door. slCome in, my man—come in,,, said the Captair. "Lottie, see if the cards are what you want. The very thing? That's all right. There's half-a- crown for you, my man." The "substitute" was turning to go, when the Captain called him back.

"Don't hurry," he said; "I just want to ask you a question or two. Rather cold work at that corner, isn't it?" "It is indeed, sir." "Ha! and not much profit attached to it, I'll be bound. How old are you?" "Fifty-five, sir." "Wife and family ? " "No, sir—quite alone—quite alone." "Ah! Ni, friends ?—and where are you going to eat your Christmas dinner P"

"Well, sir, 'through your having been so kind to me, perhaps I might take half an hour off to- morrow and go to a coffee-shop." "Oh—going to be at work to-morrow—Christmas Day ? " "Yes, sir. You see, there's cabs to go for and. things. I can't afford to lose a chance of a job these times."

"Of course not. Well, now, look here, my mar. What do you say to giving me the pleasure of yot r company to dinner here to-morrow?"

"Here, sir ? " exclaimed the messenger in astonishment.

"Yes. My wife and daughter will be delighted, I'm sure. Ah—by the bye, what's your name? "

"John Tompkins, sir."

"Ah—my wife and daughter, John Tompkim— John Tompkins, my wife and daughter. M. Tompkins, we shall be delighted to have ti e pleasure of your company to-morrow at four o'clo, k to dinner. You'll excuse the hour, but it giv the servants a longer evening."

"Thank you, sir, very much, but perhaps yolir servants wouldn't like me sitting down with them."

"Sit down with the servants! Good Heavens, man, what do you take me for? I am inviting 31( u in the true spirit of Christmas hospitality. No respectable householder sends his guests to dine in the kitchen! You will dine with us, Mr. Tompkins —you will be an honoured guest at our own little family party. Come—may we expect you?"

The man hesitated, and turned his weatherbeaten billycock about nervously in his hands. "I be your pardon, but—er—are there any other guests ? " "Yes, our next-door neighbour, Mr. Silas Finucane. You needn't mind meeting him, I assure you—a most worthy man, a great financier, and a brilliant talker. He is a bachelor and has no friends in town—that is why we have asked him; you are a bachelor and have no friends in town— that's why we ask you. Come—what do you say? " "Well, sir, I'm sure it's very good of you and very generous, and—er—if you're sure I shan't put trie ladies or Mr. Finucane out, I'll be here at four o'clock to-morrow, sir."

"That's right; and bring your appetite with you —a real old-fashioned Christmas appetite. There'll be turkey and plum-pudding and port wine and— er—Susan my dear, he sure that there are Brazil nuts. Goad-bye, Mr. Tompkins, until to-morrow."

The poor messenger made a low obeisance to the ladies, touched his brow to the Captain, and stepped out of the room.

And then Mrs. Hal Mord rose: and struggled for prerith. "Robert ! " she gasped, "are you mad? Do you mean to my that you are going to ask a person like that—a crossing-sweeper—to sit down to dinner with Its? "

"Certainly, -rny dear. I'm going to see how this beautiful Christmas story of yours works out in real life." "But Mr. 7inucane—what will he say ? "

"Oh, Finucane won't mind. He can't object, you know, because it will be Christmas Day. If he says anything, I'll lend him that Christmas story to read."

"Robert," exclaimed Mrs. Halliford, "I always knew you were peculiar, but now I think you have gone stark raving mad!"

And the domestic atmosphere became so ruffled that the Captain after dinner went in next door to smoke a cigar with Mr. Finucane, and to tell him of his little Christmas joke at his wife's expense. Mr. Finucane, who was an elegant man of five- and4hirty, and had come to London a few months previously with the reputation of having made a colossal fortune in the Colonies, said it was a splendid idea, and entered into the spirit of the thing most heartily.

An-ct then the conversation drifted on to business topics, and Mr. Finucane gave the Captain a piece of private information about a company that was to come.out immediately after Christmas, and in which he strongly recommended the Captain to have five thousand pounds.

"The shares will be worth double their issue* Tice in six Months. Hadliford. my boy." he sel/41: -only a rew or the big City itteri will ix alloweit ,11., • but as you are a particular friend of mine I'll .ttee that you get as many shares as you apply for. And that will be my Christmas present to you." Ihe Captain thought Mr. Finucane the cleverest man in money matters he had ever met, and elated with the idea of making a chair profit of five thou- .and pounds, in six months, he went home in the best of tempers ; and When the carol singers woke him up'out of his first sleep at-one o'clock in the morning he woke Mrs. Hall iford too, and-insisted on her listening to the delightfully seasonable .sentiments. And when she was just dropping off to sleep-again he Foused her once more, to wish her a Merry Christmas and a Happy New Year.

Mrs. Halliford rose on Christmas morning in anything but her usual good temper. It was most exasperating that her husband should have made such a shocking blunder as to invite's crossing- sweeper to sit down to dinner and to spend Christ- mas evening with them. Mrs. Halliford had hoped that in the happy intimacy of the domestic interior and under the genial influence of the mistletoe Mr. Fiuncane might realilie her fondest hopes and pie,- pose to Lottie. Mr. Fintwane was quite ti young man still, even for a financier, when Men are millionaires before they are forty or not at all: Ire had been most attentive both to her and to Lottie since the Captain had cultivated his acquaintance, originally made through Teedles being lost, and discovered late at • night elnit up in Mr. Finucane's greenhouse.

The Captain himself had taken an immense fancy to his next-door neighbour, who was a bright and cheery companion, and had offered the Captain the benefit of his private information to enable him to invest large sums of money in undertakings which were certain to yield a golden harvest.

Lottie had not fallen in love with the financier; but then there was that foolish flirtation of hers with Dick Lorritner—a young man with no expecta- tions. But Dick Lorritner had been forbidden the house, as such a marriage was impossible,. and he was going to South Africa, which was a blessing. This Christmas dinner-party, Mrs. Halliford had made up her mind, would bring matters to a climax. It was she who had persuaded the Captain to ask Mr. Finueane, and she had been quite extrava- gant in the matter of mistletoe. Wherever Mr. Finucane found himself with Lottie he would not have far to look to iind inspiration for a declara- tion of his feelings. •

And new the -Captain had invited that horrid crossing-sweeper who would upset el4erpliing. Once during Christmas 'periling she dveriniued to put her foot down and insist that the 'crossing-sweeper should have his dinner sent out to him on the doorstep, But the Captain was adamant. Re•was determined that his wife's theory of Christmas should be put into practice under her own roof.

The plum-pudding had been removed, and the Captain glanced around his Christmas board. Mr. Finucane, in the best of spirits, sat by Lottio and did his best to amuse her ; but Lott ie's thoughts were far away with Dick, and even when she pulled a cracker with the financier she ticla't giggle and turn her bead away raid say "Oh! John Tompkins had behaved remarlably Even Mrs. Halliford had been shamed inlo amiability towards him by his modest and carefi I conduct. He had found a fairly decent black suit somewhere, and his hands were quite clean. He really was not at all awkwarcLwith his knife and fork, and the Hallifords made up their minds that the street messenger had seen better days.

• Mr. Finucane chaffed him patronisingly, and John Tompkins said "Yes, sir; and "No, sir," and watched the brilliant . City man with undis- guised admiration. - -

At dessert the Captain made a few genial remarks. He wanted this Christmas Day to be a Christmas Number Christmas Day. In Christmas Numbers everybody told a story. Would Mr. Finucane begin? Mr. Finucane would be delighted. He twisted his black moustache, and proceeded to dazzle the company with an account of how ..one of the most famous of American "bad men," a notorious swindler and card-sharper, had kot the best of the. police in an hotel at Melbourne, and how this "bad man," Jack O'Neill, would have got clear away had it not been for a brilliant c0..7, which he. Mr. Silas Finucane, brought off alone.and unaided. The story didn't make much impression on Lottie, for whose benefit the financier was posing as a hero, but the Captain and John Tompkins were deeply interested—especially John Tompkins, who was quite carried away, and never took his eyes ofi Mr. Finucane while he was narrating his adventures. Then John Tompkins was asked to tell his story. He said modestly that he was no hand at that sort of thing, but if they would give him a little time he would try and recollect some personal adventure and tell it later on.

The gentlemen sat over their wine and smoked, Mr. Finucane presenting the street messenger with a huge cigar from his own case, and Mrs. Halli- ford and IJottie,retired to the drawing-room.

Towards eight o'clock, when mamma was,having forty winks in the armchair, Lottie stole upstairs to her bedroom, opened the window and looked out into the night for Dick.

He was there. Lottie could see him in the shadow of the opposite doorway; and just as he had crossed the road, and they were about to play a Christmas night version of the balcony scene from "Romeo and Juliet," the parlourmaid knocked at the door and said: "Please, miss, your papa wants you in the dining-room." "Wait there, Dick—wait there—l'll be back as soon as I can," Lottie called down, and the young man nodded assent. Lottie went downstairs, and found that Mrs. Halliford had preceded her to the dining-room. "Lottie, my dear," said the Captain, "Mr. Tompkins has recollected his story, and we must all hear it."

"Yes, papa," said Lottie demurely, but in her heart she wished the street messenger and his story at Bath, which was a ladylike substitute for Jericho.

"It was the gentleman's story that put mine in my head, ladies and gentlemen," sad the street messenger—"that story about Jack O'Neill, the swindler. I was a messenger over in the West End, near a club where a lot of these big card- sharpers used to go. They used to fleece young men of quality out of thousands of pounds, I've Theard say, but you would nevvr think they were rogues, for they dressed like cluls and had broughams and did everything in grand style. I suppose it got too hot at last, for one fine day the gang broke up and disappeared. I heard there were warrants out for most of them, for they'd got fifty thousand pounds at cards out of a young lord who'd just come into his estates. I recollect this Jack O'Neill. He was , pointed out to me by the commissionaire at the club door, and he had a friend who worked with him, a young man who called himself Stephen Reilly. You didn't happen to meet Reilly in Australia, did you, sir?" said the street messenger, turning to Mr. Finucane.

"No," replied Mr. Finucane. "I did not." "Ah—well, he was about the worst of the gang, but he was the cleverest—the things they said about him would make your blood run cold; but he was such a quiet one in his ways you'd never have sus- pected him. "After the gang had gone, the commissionaire at the club told me that one of the big detectives at Scotland-yard, Inspector Jones, would have given ten years of his life to have taken Reilly. He had a warrant for him the night before he got away. There was a bit of feeling in the case, you see, for this Reilly had injured a pal of Jones for life some time before in a race-course row up North, but it could never be brought home to him." "Al)," said Mr. Finucane, "I can quite under- stand that the detective was savage when he found the bird flown."

"Yes; it was a chance missed, for this Reilly was so clever at altering his appearance he was very hard to spot. He'd be a heavy-bearded old gentleman one day, and a dapper young fellow with a moustache the next. They say he lied once for six months in a Swiss town as an English clergy- man, persuaded a young lady to elope with him, got her jewellery, and left he at the -qilway station.

"JAI, t taxes more than dcotland-yard dete- tiye to catch a clever fellow like that" said Mr. Finucane, with a slight sneer. "Not always!" exclaimed the crossing-s'resper, springing up. "Stephen Reilly, I arrest you on a warrant, charged with defrauding Lord William Ratcliff of the sum of fifty thousand pounds—l am Inspector Jones, of Scotland-yard."

Before the astonished Hallifords conld utter a ivord the crossing-sweeper had seized Finucane by the wrists and slipped the handcuffs on. "I suspected A when I saw you accidentally in the City a week ago. I paid old Joe at the corner to let me take his berth that I might watch your house. Nobody came to it of the old set, and I was giving the job up when I got the invitation to dine here. I thought I'd have a good chance of looking at you close, and I accepted. When you told the story of Jack O'Neill I was sure you were mv man. Come alone. Cantain Halliford. I'll ea-u upon ybu to-morrow and tell you the finish ot my story then."

It was five mites after the door had closed on Inspector Jones and the financier before any of the family found their tongues. _ _ _ _ _

Then Lottie said quickly: "0 papa, it was for that man's sake you refused to let me marry Dick Lorrimerl Dick's outside now—mayn't he come in?"

"Good gracious!" said the Captain. "Finucane is the notorious Stephen Reilly, and to-morrow I was to have given him my cheque for five thousand pounds to invest for me—and your mother thought he would make you a first-class husband! What an escape we've had! Dick's outside? Call him in, Lottie—call him in. He mustn't go to South Africa! You shall marry him when you like. He's a most excellent young man!"

Lottie flew to the front door, an the astonished Dick was beckoned in. They didn't go into the dining-room at once. There was a huge bunch of mistletoe hanging in the hall. It had been put there for Silas Finuc,a.ne. It was under that that Lottie Halliford explained things to Dick Lorrimore.

"Susan, my dear," said the Captain to his wife, as they retired to rest that night, "you were quite right about that Christmas story. It was abso- lutely true to life, and the moral was excellent. See what has happened to us through asking a homeless crossing-sweeper to join our family circle on Christmas Day!" [ Tux _END.

OLD JOE'S 'SUBSTITUTE

BY GEORGE R. SIMS,

Author of "The Ten Commandments," "Memoirs of Mary Jane," "Rogues and Vagabonds," "As it was in the Beginning," ece., &e.

At the corner of Westbourne-terrace Lottie alliford stopped and held out her hand to her nompanion.

'Good-bye, Dick," she said. "I can't kiss you because there's that horrid man who runs messages and sweeps the crossing staring at us. But (.41, Dick, dear, dear Dick, they shall never make me marry Mr. Finucane!" - Dick Lorrirner clasped the little hand and kept it. "I'm not going down to Southampton till Boxing Day, Lottie," he said. "I must see you once again before I go. To-morrow's Christmas Day; can I see you then somewhere; only for one moment ? " "I can't come out by myself," said Lottic. "And you mustn't call; papa would be furious." "Well, can't you look out of your bedroom window, say, at eight o'clock? I'll be on the opposite side of the road. Think, I may be years in South Africa."

"Yee, Dick, I'll do it," said Dottie. "Work, Dick, work and make a fortune,' and I'll wait for you, and nobody else shall ever marry me. I swear it, Dick."

"God bless you Lottie, my darling. Good-bye."

There were tears in the eyes of the lovers as the final hand-clasp came, and Dick Lorrimer, a hand- some young fellow of five-and-twenty, turned and watched Lottie out of sight and groaned aloud in the bitterness of his despair. "I wish this infernal Finucane was at the bottom of the sea," he said. "If he had never got on the blind side of Captain lialliford I should have been, engttged to Lottie now, and the Captain would never have discovered that four hundred a year is not a proper income for a young man to get married on."

Three hours later Captain Halliford sat with his feet on the fender, a pipe in his mouth and a Christmas number in his hand. The Captain, who had retired from the merchant service when the death, of a relative left him with a totally unex- pected fortune, still retained many of his nautical ways. 'Among them was a habit of speaking his mind in terms as brief and emphatic as possible. . The Captain had been watched with considerable interest for some time past by his wife and daughter, who were busy at the table directing en- velopes for the Christmas cards which lay spread out before them.

The Captain vats undoubtedly interested in the story he was reading, but during its perusal he. Indulged in a series of grunts and snorts, which, to his womenfolk, accustomed to his moods, said, as as possible, that he was not in sympathy with the author whose work he was perusing. Lottie and I recommended it to him as such a nice story," said Mrs. Halliford under her breath.

"Anil so it is, mamma," replied Lottie. "It Made the tears come into my eyes." The Captain closed the magazine with an angry snap, and flung it from him. He intended to throw it into an easy chair just opposite to him, but the aim was a bad one, and the book fell on n white Angora cat who was lying curled up on the hearth- rug. •

The cat jumped up in terror and leaped on the table for safety. It failed to remember the proverb of looking before you leap, and, colliding with a vase of flowers that stood in the centre, upset it. The next moment Lottie Halliford was making frantic but unavailing efforts to save her Christmas cards from a watery grave.

"Really, Robert!" exclaimed Mrs. Halllford, with as much asperity in her voice as her plump amiability would allow. "I beg your pardon, my dear," said the Captain, '"it'a all the fault of that confounded talc. I could kick myself for wasting an afternoon over it.". "I wish you had kicked yourself, pupa!" s‘tliffed Lottie, tearfully. "Not hard, of course, to hurt you. That would have been much more sensible than half killing poor Teedles and quite ruinirg my Christmas cards."

-"Never mind, Lottie," said the Captain, kindly, "I'll go out and buy you some more. Poor . old Teedles, did I frighten you, then? " The sea Captain rose and stroked Teedles gently, and Teedles instantly arched his bacic and rubbed his head against his master's coat sleeve in token of complete forgiveness.

Then the Captain laid his hand soothingly on his daughter's head, but Lottie Halliford didn't arch her back or purr. She arched her eyebrows and pursed her lips and pointed to the washed ort sentiments of peace and goodwill and kindly Christmas greetings which had been printed in Germany for circulation in the homes of Great Britain.

"All right, my dear, don't let that worry you. Put on your things and we'll go to Westbourne- grove and buy the beat that can be got for money." _ _ _

• Mn. Halliford went to the window and looked out into the street.

"Lottie mustn't go; it's beginning to snow again," she said, "and I think it's most unwise of you, Robert, to venture out in such weather. You say your rhuematism is worrying you again, and this is just the afternoon to give you you: death." The Captain went to the window and surveyed the outlook. The snow had been falling all the morning, and the traffic had churned it into slush. The pavement was an inch thick in mud: "11'm," said the Captain, "it is not an inviting afternoon. I'll send one of the servants for the cards." '

"Impossible," said Mrs. Halllford. "Cook's been complaining all day that she has too much to do ; Jane has had leave to go out and buy her own Christmas presents, and Ellen. has a bad sore throat which makes me exceedingly uneasy. You never know what sore throats are going to turn to, and if she's laid up to-morrow I'm sure I don't know what we shall do. You can't get a new parlour- maid in on Christmas Day." Lottie Halliford said nothing, but sat and gazed mournfully at her Christmas cards. She had delayed posting them until the last moment in spite of the Postmaster-General's earnest entreaties. She had a sentimental objection to the Christmas cards which arrived in advance in order to oblige a lot oflpeople at St. Martin's-le-Grand who cer- tainly would not put themselves out of the way to oblige her. And now it vvas four o'clock on the afternoon of I.lhristmas Eve and the cards which she had spent quite an hour at Whiteley's in choosing were im- possible as compliments. To send any one of them Ao a friend would be be an insult.

The Captain, like all men 3vho have been accus- tomed to command, speedily lost his temper when circumstances conspired against the accomplishment of his plans. He was just beginning to betray the otate of his feelings in a little nautical language, diluted out of consideration for his wife and daughter, when he caught sight of a wo-begone, middle-aged man, clad in a ragged overcoat, who was pacing up and down on the opposite side of the road, evidently suffering acutely from the keenness of the north-easterly blast. On his right arm the man wore a brass badge, and the Captain recognised him at once as the licensed messenger who had.recently taken up the crossing and the position at the corner of the street wb'-‘ had Dreviomdy

W nen the new man came on and explained to inquirers that Old Joe was laid up with bronchitis, and he, the new-comer, was doing "Substitute" for him, everybody in Westbourne-terrace was sorry. They knew "Old Joe," and would have trusted him with the proverbial "untold gold," but they knew nothing of the "substitute, " and so he found business exceedingly slack, and frequently deserted his windy corner to pace up and dawn in front of the houses. The exercise served the double purpose of restoring his circulation and of reminding the nobility and gent?, of the neighbourhood that he was " disengaged. ' - - - - .

! When tl-143 Captain ca:mght sight of old Joe's "Substitute," his wrath was suddenly appeased, the °lack cloud passed from his brsw, and his eyes were mice more blue.

PADGATE.

SCHOOL CHILDREN ENJOY THEMSELVES.—An event which is always eagerly looked forward to by the chil- dren attending the National school is the annual dis- tribution of prizes. There was a large gathering at this year's distribution, which took place in the school- room on Tuesday afternoon. At the outset of the proceedings a number of school songs were sweetly rendered by the scholars. Lily Holcroft, a little girl aged three years, recited a piece called Santa Claus.' -Sunshine and Shower" was excellently recited by Lucy Cosgrove, as was also a poem entitled "The Venture- some Mouse," by Alice Shallcross. Mrs. Pollock-Hill presented the prizes the majority of which were for regular attendance. The following gainedlull attend- ance prizes for never having been absent during , the year, viz :—Joseph Malpas, Joseph Stringer, John Plumb, Wilfred Fryar, Samuel Malpats Stanley Houghton, Ernest Stringer, George Andrews, Thomas H. Blackwell, Aaron Taylor, William Heath, William Newall Grounds Harold Pass,Afargaret Taylor, Edith Smith, Alice Heath, Florrie Stringer, and, Ethel B. Grounds, while the following infants were also successful in gaining full prizes :—EthelAntrobus,Harry Holcmft. Harold Lovelady, and Horace Pass. The children of P.C. Heath came in for special mention, as his daughter Alice. had not been absent for six years, and his son William for five years. Altogether 78 prizes were awarded for attendance. The special prizes were afterwards pre- sented: The drawing prize (presented by, Mr. Edwin Allen), Richard Critchlev ; needlework prize (presented by Mrs. W. Harris Wright), Emma Malpas • extra needlework prize (presented by Mrs. Blackwell), Alice Heath; religious knowledge prizes pre- sented by the Vicar), Group 1., Dorothy C. London; group IT., James Goodfellow; group 111, Mabel Turner. On the motion of Mr. J. D. Buckton, a hearty vote of thanks was accorded to Mrs. Pollock-Hill for distributing the prizes, and to the Vicar for presiding. A Christmas tree (kindly provided , by Mr. G. Mather) was then lighted up and presented a pretty sight. Mrs. R. Denham distributed a packet of sweets and an orange to each child, and after a round of hearty applause had been given for the teachers "God save the King" was sung, and the proceedings terminated.

THE, STOCKTON HEATH TRAMWAY. . --- Objectioos to the Order.

Saturday last was the latest day on which objec- tions could he laid with the Board of Trade against the Warrington Corporation Light Railway Order. It appears that objections have been sent in by the Cheshire County Comacil and the Parish Council of Stockton Heath. The County Council objection is on the ground of the insufficient widening of Old London- road and the omission from the order of certain agreed clauses between the County Council and the Warring- ton Corporation wlich the Light Railway Com- missioners refused to insert. The most important of these clauses relates to the paving of the proposed --tra-ek with setts, a work which the Corporation are I quite willing to undertake. I The Parish Council objections have reference to the • width of ths footpaths opposite St. Thomas's Church I and the proposed construction of a double line of i rails.

This objection will no. doubt cause further delay in the proposed tramway to Victoria Square, Stockton Heath as in all probability the Board of Trade will hold a formal inquiry into the objections.

A Woman's Large Heart. An inquest was couductefil at the Warrington Police Station on Saturday by Mr. S.Brighouse,County Coroner touching the death ot Mary Ann Farrell,who lodged in Oliver-street, and was found dead in an ashpit on Thursday of last week.—Deceased's husband stated ' that he last saw her alive about a fortnight ago. Owing to her drunken habits he had not lived with her for about five years.—Catherine Doherty said deceased had lodged with her for about three weeks. On Wednesday afternoon she came to the house drunk and in a filthy craidition. Witness turned her outland told her,to go to the Workhouse.—Charles Davies stated that at seven o'clock. on Thursday morning he found deceased lying dead in an ashpit in Stoney- yard and sent for the police.—Dr. Smith said he had made a post-inortem examination of the body which was very ill-nourished, there being an almost total absence of fat. There were no marks of violence. The heart was iu an advanced state of disease and was I about twice the natural size. It weighed over a pound, whereas the normal weight was only eight ounces. The cause of death was failure of the heart's action.—A verdict of "Death from natural causes" was returned.

Current Cut off. The officials of the Tramways Departnaent have reason to congratulate themselves upoti the fact that very few hitches have occurred in the working of the service since its inauguration. There was a slight mishap on Sunday afternoon which occasioned con- siderable inconvenience, the current being cut off for about half an hour. .one of the cars was proceeding along the Sankey Bridges section when the trolley arm by some means canie off the, wires, and striking one a the guard wires above, snapped it. The broken wire came into contact_ with the live wire, with the result that the current was cut off, the circuit not being complete. After about half an hour's wait the stop- age was confined to the Sankey Bridges section, and. :p ortly afterwards the defect was remedied all along elle routes.
