CHRISTMAS is always Still a SeasMerrimenton of "Merry Christmas," by whatever unfavourable circumstances it may be surrounded. The tradition keeps its ground; the old associations 'play their well-established part; the mirth is kept up at the family gatherings, even though these may contain. sad and despairing hearts. The happiness of the may be transient, but for the time being it is very real. "The-trde of time- flows hack for us," said the late POET-LAURRATE. The tide of depressed spirits, of deepening apprehensions, of grief over what is past, and of fear for what the immediate future may conceal, is barred out at the Christmas season. We erect a sort of Nile data against it. The turbulent and swelling waters are not permitted to rush in again through the sluipes until the New Year has opened. What is painful can wait; whatever is unlucky can be concealed ; whatever is threatening can be ignored. There must be nothing to interfere with the general joy. And Thursday, we do not doubt, was the happiest day of the year that will shortly close. All over the land there were delighted family reuniens. On the day that has just passed these must have had a more than ordinary, and in some thousands of eases a really thrilling, interest. There was a clo;e personal concern in the late war in almost every English household. If the father, or the brother, or the son, was not env,agel in that unfortunate conflict, there was certain to be some 'relative on the veldt. Many of these have now come home ; they have spent their first Christmas since the fighting was concluded with those who love them best and whom they best love The safe return, we may be sure, gave a new radiance to the holly and a new beauty to the mistletoe. To many thousands of hearts this has been the happiest Christmas that ever was. Those have come back who, a year ago, were never expected to return. They have come back, in so many cases, well and hearty, with browned and smiling faces,and with a store of good tales to tell. They have come back in others maimed acd broken; but still they have come hack, with the old love in their hearts finding the old response in the hearts of others. And some have not come back at all. Oh! those that have not come back are to be counted in drearily saddening numbers; but those whcf have lost them are proad of them, and the Christmas festivities must have lightened even 'a sorrow like theirs. And yet it is not a good Christmas season that we are passing through. The children, of course, will be delighted with the festive season for what it has brought them. SANTA CLAUS will have come down the, chimney in the night, with how- ever humble a token of his regard for the young. Something will be in the stocking, however small its value may prove to be ; and this Christmas will be remembered in years to come as one of those seasons that seem to have been richer, and better, and fuller than all those by which it was followed. The imagination of the young has a learvellous transforming power. What it looks lAA' seems to have been richer than the treasures Of ALADDIN. This has, nevertheless, been the first hard Christmas for many years past. Tee weather has most happily been mer2iful.lt has been possible to proceed with work that it usually impeded at this time of the year by frost and snow. The expected pinch has not introduced itself into many households which are accustomed to it at this season There has been a goose on the table which ordinarily, at Christmas time, holds nothing much better than the dinner of DUKE HUMPHREY. A mild Christmas has diffused happiness over many an English heart'i. It happens, notwith- standing, that this has been the most pinched

The Examiner, Friday, December 26, 1902.

Christmas for something like;ten years. The high tide of national prosperity has suddenly fallen back. The big wages, supplemented by overtime, are, no longer paid. A nation which has been living bountifully on its capital is finding, as it has found many a time before, that war is only temporarily good for trade. Tnere has risen a cry of distress from our smallest towns and cur grestest cities. The demand of the moment is that we should feed the poor and succour the distressed. Perhaps the cry has been raised too soon. If that should not be the case there is a black outlook before us, which Heaven forefend. What we have to fear, however, is a collapse of our industries; and the apprehension is based on only too numerous indicatims of what is to follow. We shrink, however, from striking la dismal note at a time like this. The crisis may be temporary ;, it may easily be bridged over by those who have prospered most in the fat years; it may come to an end in the year that is shortly to be rung in by 'the church bells. The Christmas season, at any rate, is a time for that hopefulness which is called optimism,' a period at which we may well be excused for expecting too much of the future, bat hardly at all for casting, as some poet has said" shadow o'er the days to come."

READERS of Stevenson's The Dead Archbishop. works will remeruber how, looking steadfastly OR guest, one of his characters says, " You are a strange physician." The guest makes reply, "I am a natural law, and people call me D nth." And to this comes the unexpected sequel: " Why did you not tell me so at first? I have been waiting for you these many years. Give me your hand, and welcome." The thought seems to tit in with the career of the deceased PRIMATE. He has parsed sway in old age, under the inevitable law of nature, but there has been no quivering nor repining at the prospect. On the contrary, he has looked on life and death equally with a cheerful if sometimes rather grim courage, and nothing could shew more thoroughly his sense of the transient nature of our existence than his horror of waste of time. There were people who thought him occesionally churlish in his anxiety to .be rid of them. Those who understood him were able to forgive this apparent roughness, in their. satisfaction at the good-heartedness of the ,man. DR. Temenz's advance to the Primacy'was the upward move- ment of sheer merit. He started without any social advantages, and he certainly did not make any for himself. His father was a poor military officer, for 'a period Governor of Sierra Leone, and all he could do for his boy was to give him a good education at BLIINDELL'S school. Of that the lad availed him- self to the fullest extent, and his entrance to Balliol excited great hopes. But nobody then sus- pected that the Devonian school was to be the bottom rung of a kind of Jaeos's ladder leading Up tolLambeth Palace. At Balliol, TEMPLE was brought into contact with men who were to be leaders of thought, but the influence was hardly favourable. They were men of greet breadth of view; and breadth of view in • matters •of theology is often misconstrued as meaning heresy and schism. We know that at Rugby the scholar, as headmaster, approved himself in the estima- tion of the public. But he joined with other Oxford Broad Churchmen in publishing a volume of Essays and Reviews, and excited so much indignation that an attempt was made to eject him from his office. We recall these events because they evidence that his triumph was one of strength of character and indifference to worldly gain. His appointment to, the 5.30 of Exeter brought a storm of indignation about the head of MT. GLADSTONE, who dared At; but from that time forth Dr. TEMPLE quietly asserted his force in the Church. The West Country people liked his vigour, his obvious sincerity,the rugged eloquence of his speech, and his complete indifference to worldly considerations. The dissensio a softened into approval, and finally to a real feeling at sorrow when be was translated, in 1885, to preside over the diocese of London. With the nature of his work in the metropolis, and in the last six years as Primate, all are familiar. It may be that he was respected more than he was loved. In later years his splendid combat with old age excited the admiration of all who knew. of the 'stress he endured. His last speech in the House lof Lords was nearly as tragic se that of Chatham. He was compelled te leave it unfinished, and that appeal that the Church should not suffer the Nonconformists to have any hardship or grievance in the working of the Education Act was aelivered on his behalf by other lips. Ibis pleasant to think, however, this speech was in the nature of an eirenicon,and that his earlier speeches frankly acknowledged the grounds. for the Non- conformist sense of injaitice in the single-school area. He was zealous for his Church, but he was anxious also that nowhere should the privilege which the State gave to denominational teaching be strained. _

Historians will donbtless compare Archbishop BENsox and Archbishop TEmpts,and wonder how both could have touched the public sentiment so largely. The truth is, they had more in common than appears on the surface. Both were strenuous devoted workers, who cared little for themselves in the struggle to fulfil their engagements. We know. that Dr. TEMPLE, at his beat, thought , nothing of working fifteen •hours out of twenty ; that he became a teetotaller in midd'e ; and was indifferent to all pleasure except , necessary rest from labour. We know that Dr. BiNsoN would take the utmost pains to touch up a letter to a private correspondent so as to convey exactly what he wanted to t av,and that his mind was engrossed by the task he had before him. These &hers of men anglad in differ- ent fashions. But they were as devout, as faith- ful, and as humble as the disciples of old. They recognised that life is a stewardship of which a strict account most be given, and they were free of any suspicion of vanity or self-consciousness. The Episcopate has rarely been so lacking in eminent men as it is to-day; and the loss of Dr. TEMPLE, with his acorn of sycophancy, sham, and unreality, is a serious one. But we may hope that, as CARLYLE says, the occasion will produce the men. The Church has need of some robust, broad-minded intellectuals, also end owed with capacity for sympathy with the social difficulties , of the twbntieth century.

The National Association for the Suppression of Bad Language has a title which is not calculated to impress one with ics businesslike methods, but its objects are such as must commend themselves to all geed citizens. It is stated that the Sneiety as received sympathetic letters from the King, and many ether eminent personages, and if it can do anything to purify the streets of this revolt- ing nuisance it will merit and receive the thanks

of everybody whose good opinion is worth having. IThe most frequent offenders in the use of obscene language are drunken or half-drunken men, and the Association should seek to move public opinion in the direction of more drastic treatment of these men at the hands of the police and the magis- trates. The man or woman who steals by reason of want ie surely less an enemy of the public than the half-drunken ruffian, who pollutes the air with foul oaths, yet it is the uniform practice to deal much more severely with the former than with the latter.

Some local societies are extremely fortnnaie in the selection of their presidents. The Warring- ton and District Agricultural Society is one of them. Not only does this society possese in Sir. Gilbert Greene!l a president who takes a promin- ent and active part in the managemeat of the society bat he personally keeps it in a sound financial position. Last year's loss on ths show at Walton Park was £285 2s. 10d. and to many a society such a heavy deficit would have been a knock-out blow. But in. Warrington it is differ- ent. Sir Gilbert Greenell has come to the rescue with a special donation of £3OO ana the hon. treasurer has the felicity of showing a balance in hand with which to commence the ensuing year. With such a Presidont at the head of affairs it is not surprising that the trieinb6re should unani- mously re-appoint him at the annual meeting on Monday last.

It is a far cry to the next show in August 1903, but the Committee are already drawing up their prospectus. Several important alterations have been decided upon. There is to be an additional class for hunters, and the prizes for tra !Osman's or tenant farmers' turnouts are to be incr.,asecl in number. Classes for polo ponies will also form a new feature of next year's show, which promises to be of an unusually interestipg charActe.e. It has been found necessary to exclude eggs, dressed poultry and potatoes from the list of exhibits on the ground that "these produce classes have not proved either interesting or useful." The com- mittee have issued an appsal for that " general support which they consc entiously ask for from the immediate neighbourhood." Warrington, it is truly pointed out, is a great agricultural centre midway between Liverpool and Manchester, Preston and Crewe, and ought to be the pivot round which agriculturists should assemble. To a great extent they do, but there is room for con- siderable improvement. The Warringtori and District Agricultoral Society has done much to further the interests of agriculture, which after all,is the greatest industry in the country, and we hope the day is far distant when its usefulness will be crippled through lack of sapport.

SAD CHRISTMAS INCIDENT AT I THE RUNCORN VOLUNTEERS. ST. HELENS. Fatal Carelessness with a Gun. Farmer Censured by the Coroner.

_,_Mr. Brighouse, county coroner, held the inquest at WhistOn Workhouse, on Wednesday, on John Flaherty, aged twenty-three, farm Jabourer, of Kemble-street, Prescot, who died in the workhouse infirmary on Mon- day night after being shot in the thigh the previous night by Mt. William Joseph Woods, a farmer, of Watery-lane, Becleston, St. Helens. • The Coroner inquired from Woods if he was legally represented. _ _

--cir-o-o-ds-iSiid he had not thought he wbuld require counsel. . _

The Coroner: It would have been far more satisfac- tory if you had been represented. You have shot a man. I shoula think that was sufficiently grave to justify you in seeking legal assistance. Mary Melia, widow, of 25,Kemble-street,said deceased lodged at her house about three months. She thought he was twenty-three years of age. The Coroner.: I have it as thirty-two. _

Police-constable Huhne : I "apparently about thirty-tivo." Mrs Melia said her daugllter knew about , The daughter then came in, but she confased matters still more by saying that deceased, positively told her that his name was Flannigan. Police-constable Hultne, after some discussion, said that he had seen a letter in the man's box- from a cousin, who addressed him as "my dear 'cousin, John Flaherty." The Coroner: Then we will stick to Flaherty: Edward Arkwright, farm labourer, said he was plough- ing in one field and deceased and Mr. Woods were ploughing in the field alongside of itAbout two o'clock in the afternoon deceased called to him, and he went into their field and had two drinks of whisky with them. Deceased was a bit fresh and Mr Woods had had a drop. Mrs. Mary Ann Woods said her husband came into the house about four o'clock on Mondayafternoon and took a gun out' of the corner of the kitchen. He was then the worse for drink, fend she said to him "Be careful with the gun." She would not have said that if she had not noticed that he had had some drink. He did notspeak at all, but simply rushed out of the door as if he was in a hurry: Five or six minutes after she heard a loud report, and then a avian. She rushed out and heard her husband shout. She saw Flaherty who was just falling. Deceased moaned terribly and she ran screaming for help. Her husband went to the man, and tried to get hold of the leg where the blood wasflowing. A man named George Stocss came from the next field, and, with a man named• Hayes, they bandaged the legiap tightly, and he was.taken off to the hospital. Her hus- band said that deceased had said he saw a hare that used to come and eat the cabbage and celery, and he wanted to go for the gun, but that he (Woods) rushed in before him and got it. The coroner examined two guns that were produced. The gun which did the mischief belonged to,Woods's brother.

said in the report

Woods said if it had been his own gun that he was handling it would not have happened, but he tad no cartridges that would fit his own gun. • Mrs. Woods said deceased and her husband were on very good terms, and Flaherty had arranged to come and live at the farm. Woods gave evidence, and said that deceased brought a full bottle of whisky, aud they drank it. When they had put the horses in the stable, deceased said the hare had come again, and witness rushed in for the gun. When he returned, it had gone. They had been talking about cartridges and deceased could not understand how No. 10 cartridges were larger than No. 12's. He showed him the cartridge in the gun, and as he was °losing the breach the gun went off. He had a great liking for deceased, and would rather it had been himself that had been shot.

Dr. Haslam spoke to the man dying at a quarter-past eleven on Monday night. He described the wound in the thigh, and said it was quiteconsistent with the state- ments of Woods. The jury returned a verdict of "Death from mis- adventure," but asked the coroner to censure Woods for his carelessness. The Coroner did this, and told Woods he had made two mistakes. First, he got too much to drink; and, secondly, he had not realised that a gun was thp most dangerous instrument possible to handle without the greatest care.

NOTES ON GAMES AND ATHLETICS. -- Rugby Football. By " Ovalist."

It was hard on the Warrington crowd. on Saturday last to have to witness a defeat by Bradford on the first occasion in seven weeks that the first team had played at home. Most people felt despondent when they heard the news that Isherwood and Fish would be absentees, as the only hope of beating the burly Bradfordians lay in superior back play. There was sympathy with the Captain but ncine with Fish, Who has had so many permits that Nemesis was bound to overtake him eventually. He is too fond of work;ng just the absOlute minimum and no more, and has aroused the natural suspicions of the professional sub-committee. Anyhow Warrington lost and it was pretty clear during • the, game that the result would probably have been different had Fish and Isherwood been in their places.

There is no blame io be attached to the forwards, Who mended their damaged reputations by a fine dis- play. True, they were the heaviest eight Warrington could put in the field. but they were still much lighter than Bradford, and did uncommanly well to get, pos- session so often. Where Virarrington's weakness lay was in the back division, which seemed entirely deficient in scoring power. Harris is far from being in form, and made no sort of progress towards the line on Saturday. and several times he failed to stop Dtmbavin'when the latter. was getting awe'.'. Dicken- son and Hallam were the only two men who excelled themselves, and each played superbly from start to finish. Knight did by no means badly, Ind. Bro-ikes was occasionally at fault, though he tackled well. The halves showed some slight improVement and Passed the ball out from the scrummage better than

Bradford won by 10 points to nil,—a score that hardly represents the Play. Their • first try was a doubtful one. ‘Then, soon after half-time _Warrington Made a great effort and Burgess forced his 'way over, only to have the try disallowed by a referee who was already' unpopular with the Warrington crowd., viz. : Mr. Farrar, of Halifax. The point seemed a perfectly legitimate one and the spectators at that part of the field hooted until the referee stopped the game and refused to proceed while the noise continued. The finishing touch was put on by Sinton who ran very speedily and scored cleverly from rlagers' pass. Bradford were best represented by Mosby, Sint= and Ward, the last-named never making a mistake on the defence. Broadley played half-back, but was upended too smartly to get really dangerous, though he gave , his threecrnarters plenty of work.

WARRINGTON.

Personal Lord and Lady Derby will have a large 'ftmily party at Knowsley Park during the Christmas holidays, and there will be some battues in the extensive woods and coverts in and round the park. RUNCORN AND WIDNES.

CAMDEN SALE OF WORK.

The amount raised by the above sale of work and Christmas Tree was the very satisfactory sum of £BO. On Friday afternoon Mrs. Morris opened the sale of work, the result being £25, Thursday yielding £55.

CFIESHIRECOUNTY COUNCIL AND THE EDUCATION BILL. A special meeting of the above Council will be held at the Crewe Arms Hotel, Crewe, on Friday next, at 11 o'clock in the forenoon, for the purpoSe of considering the Education Act, 1902, and to take suchaction thereon as may be deemed advisable in order to bring the same into operation.

THE FLOODING OF RUNCORN FOOTBALL GROUND. On Wednesday and Thursday the heavy rain experi- enced the last few days completely flooded the Runcorn football ground. Though the water gradually dis- appeared it left the ground a quagmire in certain parts, and the "A" team match with Runcorn Hornets fixed for Saturday had to be abandoned. Another date will probably be given to the Hornets. The drainage arrangements of the field are being overhauled, and the ground will be in order for the next engagement. otu 4

A SHOCKING CASE OF NEGLECT.,

At the Runcorn Police Court oliFriday, before Messrs F. J. Norman (in the chair) and E Imison, Catherine Rourke, Handley's-court,Runcorn, was charged upon a warrant with having on December 18th and other days during the last six months neglected her four children in a manner likely to cause them unnecessary suffering and injury to health. She elected to be dealt with summarily and pleaded not guilty.

RUNCORN SONS OF TEMPERANCE. The half-yearly meeting of the Lifeboat Crew Division of this order was held on Saturday evening in the Greenway-road school. There was a large attendance of officers and members. The President, Mr. Thomas Clarke, presided. The election of officers resulted as follows :—President, Mr. George Powell; associate, Mr. Edgar Wilkinson; recording scribe, Mr. Edward Key, jun.; financial secretary Mr John Cooke (theretiring secretary, Mr. James Johnson, has left the town), assistant secretary, Mr. Thomas Dutton; conductors, Messrs. JoSeph Walker and Henry Millington; sentinels, Messrs. J. Howard and Lea; chaplain, Mr. Thomas R. Whyte; treasurer, Mr. Daniel Bisbrown; sick stewards, Messrs. Henry Millington and Joseph Walker; auditors, Messrs. George Powell and Samuel Saunders; trustees, Messrs. Thomas Heesom. S. M. Temperley and John Leathwood. Powell, Thomas Dutton,Jolm Cooke, Thomas Clarke and Edward Key, jun.

EARLESTOWN AND NEWTON.

RESCLT OF A DRUNKEN BRAWL. About half-past one on Monday morning a man named Edward Golding, of St. Helens, was found by P.C. Lynskey lying on the footpath in Crow-lane, Newton, having a severe wound on the head. He was taken to Dr. Latham's where his wound was dressed, and he was afterwards removed home. From inquiries made it was found be had been in a drunken brawl, and had received his injuries in consequence.

NEWTON URBAN DISTRICT 'COUNCIL.

An extraordinary meeting of the D strict Council was held in the Board Room at the Town Hall, Earlestown. on Tuesday evening. Mr. T. Ormond (vice chairman) presided. The meeting was called for the purpose of authorising an application to the Local Government Board for a Provisional Order, to commend Section 53 of the Newton District Improvement Act 1855, so that t shall be . lawful for the Council to borrow, or re- borrow with the sanction of the Local Government Board, on the credit of the district fund, general im- provement rate and highway rate, such sums as may be required from time to time for the purpose of discharg- ing expenses incurred or to be incurred by them in the execution of the said Act. (b) To amend Act lof the Newton-in-Makerfield Provincial Order, 1901, so that the securities to be given for the repayment of any sum or sums borrowed or to be borrowed under that order for the purposes of the Council's water undertaking shall be the revenue of the water undertaking, thewater rates under the Local Act, and the district fund, and general improvement rate. The applications were authorised. THE CHRISTNIA.S SHOW AT EARLESTOWN. _

Notwithstanding the fact that times at Earlestown are not quite so good as they were at this season last year, there is a 'plentiful display of seasonable goods in the shop windows, and buyers seem to be fairly plentiful. All the grocery establishments are well stocked with the usual goods generally on show at Christmas time, 'and there is no lack of choice. In the drapery shops there are tempting displays of articles suitable for pre- sents,whilst the butchers are to the front with shows of prize beasts sheep and pork. Of feathered stuff there is plenty, and nobody need travel beyond the town to stock themselves with all the requisites for the festive season. To please the children fancy goods and toys of all descriptions are on exhibition and judging from the parcels one sees in the arms of the youngsters they le not by any means being forgotten.

t RUNCORN NOTES.

[BV A CORRE:IPONDESTI

The Runcorn School Board have resolved to extend the Victoria-road schools pretty considerably and the plans were examined and passed at their last meeting. It only now remains for the Board of Education to ap- prove of the plans and the work will be once more pro- ceeded with. When the work is completed there will be 'unbounded satisfaction in all quarters e3paclslly among those who desire that their children should have School' Board education. The school has so long been overcrowded that it is a notorious fact that children have been compelled to go to Voluntary schools for their secular education. The proposed alt -ations have not come a bit too soon.

At the than of writing the weather is anything but Christmas-like.. It reallY appears as though the festive season will be marked by an utter absence of those climatic conditions unseparably associated with Christmastide. There can be no doubt but that the fros and snow will come before Winter's "iron sway" is o'et and it seems rather a pity that we cannot have thr English weather of the days of yore. a

M16,4,a5,.

Next day the houghs were lopped Ott close to the bole, and the great trunk divided into lengths, the largest of which was chosen for the Yule-Jog of Sorrow. And a team of sturdy young fellows set to work to drag it to The Grangt. In olden days its passage had been to the accompaniment of songs and dances; but now all that was ohanged, and the progress of the yule-log was silent and sorrowful. For it was the last Christmas Eve of the Rixtons in the land of their inheritance.

As the light fell the respectful mien of the villagers NVII S relaxed by the especial desire of the Squire himself. With the mummers came full joyousness awl sefvonable revelry. All sign of sorrow was ba.,.ished for the time. They were bidden to enter gaily into the festivities.

"For the lasir'tinie, dear," said Cyril, sadly, to his Promised bride. She kissed him.

"Who knows?" she said. "There may yet be good fortve in store for you such as you do not dream of.•

Her cheeks were flushed, and her eyes so sparkled that the young man was forced to curiosity. "Hilda, what do you mean ? " he asked. "Ah, that is my secret. I have a Christmas gift for you, Cyril dear. But you are not to have it until the yule-log is well ablaze." He said no more. He could not enter quite into the spirit of it all. But he did not wish to spoil such pleasure as the girl was able to feel. It was, he supposed, some seasonable joke or fancy that she had in store for him.

With the vicar and Sir Graham they sat down to dinner in the hall. A huge fire was lighted, and the embers of last year were placed thereon and then the huge log itself.

The flames leaped up and round, and caught the bark which crackled and threw out its showers of sparks and wreaths of smoke. By the time dinner was over it was well alight, blazing bravely up the huge chimney. Sir Graham, producing a bottle of his famous Madeira, proposed a toast: "To the last Christmas of the old race in the old place." He glanced round at the old portraits flanging there on the oaken walls ; then, draining his glass, he flung it to the ground. It—iplintered into a hundred glittering fragments. Cyril and the vicar did the same ; but Hilda, to the surprise of them all, set down her glass untasted. "Will you not drink the toast, Hilda ? " asked Cyril, at a loss to understand her. "No," she replied, "I cannot. It is too cruel, and what's more, I don't believe it." She burst out crying. "Believe what?" asked Sir Graham, touched by her grief. "Believe that you will go," she repiled. Then she dried her eyes and stood up with the glass in her hand. "Mv toast is Hone for the best.' " the said. She drant it and set uown the glass unbroken on the table.

"It is a trifle late for that, my dear," said Sir Graham.

"Oh, I don't know. lam a witch, you know, to-night—a white witch ; and I prophecy that there is nothing cerlain bnt the " "Does that mean that the Hall will still be ours, white witch?" laughed Cyril. "Perhaps, yes, perhaps no. The oracle can say no more."

"The oracle is somewhat dark," said the vicar, dismally. "Bat that is always their fashion from the Witch of Endor to the Pythoness of Delphi. Don't be foolish, Hilda."

The girl laughed and skipped round to Sir Graham, into whose ear she whispered something. Sir Graham started, and looked at his future daughter-in-law in surprise. "Impossible, my dear--" "Hush!" Hilda kissed him. "You must say , nothing, or the spell will be broken." The Baronet entered into the spirit of the jest, and laughingly refused to gratify the curiosity of the others. And, indeed, there was no time for , questions, as the moment the cloth was removed a clash of instruments outside heralded the arrival of the. rustic mummers. The gentry retreated to a dais at the bottom of the hall under the gallery, I and, at a signal, the great doors at the side were flung open. Then poured in a crowd of gay figures, such as might have flown from the land of dreams through the Ivory Gate. Robin Hood in Lincoln Green, with bow and arrow, danced along with Maid Marian, dressed in kirtle of the same hue with a wreath of may blossom. The dragon—a glittering monster roanceuvred by two men—rolled along his scaly length with St. George, armed from head to heel, in playful pursuit. Santa Claus was there with his basket of toys, the Great Turk, fierce and black-bearded, Giant Gorabuster, and King Arthur, and a crowd of impish children dressed as fairies, goblins, and fiery little devils. Nor was wanting a jester in parti-coloured garb, with cap and bells, who cracked bad jukes and slashed round freely with a bladder tied to a stick. The -village band played gay music, the mummers danced gaily, and the great yule-log, spluttering fire, roared up the wide chimney. It was a true Christmas revel, and might have been held at Windsor before King Edward himself. Then came eating and drinking for all; and it must be said that the mummers did full justice to both. There were the wassail bowl, the mince pies, the plum pudding, and of them all they made but small account. And as the night. drew on to the stroke of twelve, the fun grew fast and furious. They played kiss-in-the-ring, and blindman's buff, and single-staff. And the young folks danced their country dances, while the old sat chattering in the corners. Even Sir Graham thrsw off his melancholy for the moment. When the Fong hours came, is was he who gathered them round the yule- ' log, and bade them tell their legends and their ghost stories, until the timid ones quaked with fear, and continually looked over their shoulders into the dark corners of the huge hall. Of such tales Cyril had a wonderful store. He could-tell off spectres in the church3ard, of the wild frolics of witches on, the moors at the midnight hour, and of haunted houses and fiiiry rings. his hearers bung on his words with bated Ibreath, and when an un- usually shrill blast whistled doun the chimney, causing the yule-log to crack and sparkle, some there were who actually shrieked. But they all said that never was there so delightful a Christ was. • "And now J. ton going to tell a story," said Hilda, when Cyril's sock chewed signs of giving out., "and I think you will find my story the most jut cresting of all. " "Oh, yes, Miss Hilda. do," chorused the villagers. She was a great favourite with them all. "Is it a ghost story, Miss Hilda? " "No. It is the story of .a lost, treasure." Sir Graham heaved a sigh. and Cyril looked somewhat curiously at the girl. But she heeded neither. Her eyes were fixed on the glowing mass of wood which, assiduously stoked and stirred and poked, began to crack mind rive in all directions. One long crevice extended right down the centre of the log, and it seemed as if the tierce heat would part it in two. With her eyes anxiously 'upon it, Hilda commenced in the good old fashion of the fairy tales. "Once upon a time there lived in this very hall , a knight named Sir Philip Rixton.", A tremot of interest ran through her audience. Father and son looked at each other apprehensively. She went on:

"He was rich in land, but richer in jewels, since his grandfather, having been in the New World, had brought from there much treasure. When his son, Walter, was condemnedlor rebelling against King datnes If., a pardon was offered to him on condition' that the jewels were given to the Crown, which then annted money. Sir Philip would have surrendered the jewels, which were said to be worth fifty thousand pound, foe the sake of his son, but in an interview with his father, the young man forba43 him to do so. He was seriously wounded, and could not hope to live long; and, unwilling that his family should be ruined, he declared that .would rathe,- tw.

BEFORE THE MAGISTRATES

VIOLENT ASSAULTS ON POLICE OFFICERS AT WARRINGTON.

At the Warrington Police Court on Monday, before Mr. F. Monks and other magistrates,John Broderick, 23. Mersey-street was charged with having been drunk and disorderly in Haydock-streetat 12.5 on Sunday morning and with assaulting Police Constables Houghton and Gibbs—P.C. Houghton stated that he Was on duty in Ha.ydock-street when he heard the prisoner making use of filthy language. He told him to go home and he re- fused and witness took him into custody. Prisoner became very violent and struck him about the face and head, knockin.g his helmet off. In Winwick-street he struck witness a violent blow on the nose and kicked him about the legs. Witness blew his whistle and P.C. Gibbs came to his assistance. Prisoner was very violent on the way to the Police Station—P.C. Gibbs said that on hearing the whistle he went to P.C. Houghton's assistance and found him bleeding from the nose and coveredwith mud. Prisoner was struggling and making use of filthy language. He was very violent all the way to Bold street and when they get there he threw himself on the ground and kickedwitness on the legs several times Witness had to sit on his legs aud hold him down until he promised to go quietly. Prisoner was shouting and cursing all the way to the Police Station—in fact witness had never heard a"dirtier mouthed man"since he became a policeman—Superintendent Monaghan said that was prisoner's 46th appear ince and he was one of the worst characters the police had to deal with—A sentence of three month's imprisonment with hard labour was passed.

A BOY ROBS A SLOT GAS METER.

. A boy aged fourteen named John Langford, who resides in Haydock-street, appeared before the Waroing- ton magistrates on Wednesday on the charge of stealing from the slot gas meter at the dwelling of Edward Baker, 38, Havdock-street, between four and five o'clock' on Monday afternoon, 9s. 2d. the money of the Corpor- ; ation.—He at first pleaded not guilty but ultimately; admitted the robbery.---Superintendent Monaghan said • that at about four o'clock on Monday afternoon prose- cutor's wife left the house. She bolted the back door and latched the front door but did not lock it. On returning at five o'clock she found the furniture dis- arranged. The sofa had been placed against the front door and she had some difficulty in entering the house. The back door had been opened. On the floor was the drawer of the slot meter, a pair of broken scissors, and the lock which had been broken off the meter. Information was given to the police and sus- picion fell upon the prisoner who was spending money very freely on Monday. He was arrested, and half-a-crown in copper was found inhis possession. He had given tenpence to another boy, and he took some friends to the Royal Court Theatre in the evening. The rest of the money was spent on sweets, &c. Prisoner had been before the Court twice for theft in 1900, and once for stone throwing.—Prisoner's mother said she did not think the lad was quite responsible for his actions at times. He brought sixpence home on Mon- day and told her he had earned it—Prisoner was sen- tenced to six strokes the birch rod and ordered to ; be kept in custody until six o'clock in the evening.

PERSISTENT CRUELTY TO HIS WIFE.—John Owen, 26, Ellesmere-street, was summoned by his wife Eleanor Owen for using persistent cruelty towards her. Com- plainant sued for a separation and maintenance order.— Mr. Burton appeared for the complainant.—The evidence for the complainant showed that the parties were married on the 15th August, 1896, and had had one child. From the 11th October of the present year , defendant had used persistent cruelty towards her. He was constantly demanding money for drink and when it was not given to him he would abuse her. He had constantly locked her out. On one occasion Owen had attempted to throttle his wife but had been pulled off by his oldest stepson. He had often threatened to kill her.—His wages were 355. weekly. The defendant denied the cruelty and urged that his wife had grossly neglected the home. The bench decided to grant a separation order with a maintenance order of Ils, a week the complainant to have the custody of the youngest child.—Defendant left the court vowing to go to prison rather than pay. ALLEGED ILL-TREATMENT OF A HORSE.—Samuel Johnson, Frodsham, was summoned for ill-treating a horse on the 11th December at Frodsham. The defendant denied kicking the horse but was obliged to use his strap to prevent the horse falling four feet in the boilers.—Henry Scott, horse keeper, for thirty years, said there was nothing the matter with the horse. The case was dismissed.

===== 0002601_19021226_art0108.txt | included=true | included =====
CHARACTER SKETCHES.

Mr: Balfour.

"The worst thing about the Prime Minister," said a Liberal opponent the other day, "is that you cannot help liking him so much personally." This remark is a key to the position of the Prime Minister in the House of Commons. Mr. Balfour .is literally a man ithout an enemy. Howeier high political passion may run in the heat of some bitter debate, every attack is a fair political thrust—every hit is well above the belt. And his political attitude is the direct outcome of his temperament--he is by nature courteous, kindly, slightly cynical, slow to resent, quick to forgive. If a ballot were taken in the House for the most popular Member, the Prime Minister would probably be at the head of the poll. Moreover, he is certainly one of the best speakers In Parliament. and no man can intervene in debate with better results to his own side. His speech in defend- ing the Kevon Slaney amendment against the High Church attack has hardly been surpassed since Mr. Gladstone disappeared from the Parliamentary arena. H e was hardly intended, however, by nature for a political leader ; and it may be suspected that, if the secrets of his heart could be read, he is held to the exhausting task chiefly by a keen sense of public duty. His natural role in life, if what he regards as an imperative obligation did not call him, would be that of an affectionate thinker, a lover of music, and a man who finds perhaps his greatest pleasure in standing before the fire after dinner, sur- rounded by a devoted family and a choice circle of enthusiastic friends, talking lightly, but thoughtfully, of the great and little problems of life and thought. He obviously finds it difficult to give his mind to details which do not interest him. Like Emerson, he thinks that "details are of the snake."

===== 0002601_19021226_art0109.txt | included=true | included =====
Sir Henry Campbell-Bannerman.

Every word that has been said about the Prime Minister's geniality and kindness might be truthfully repeated of Sir Henry Campbellßannerman. He is a type of the "very parfait " Scottish gentleman. At a time when he was the object of the preposterous insults of a section of his opponents, and was keenly feeling the inability of some of his followers to support him in one or two divisions, not a word of anger or resentment escaped him. Indeed, if one ventured a criticism of his public atti- tude, it would be that he sometimes shows too much courtesy te opponents who can neither understand nor appreciate it. If he had lashed out on one or two occa- sions when he was treated -by some of his opponents with an insolence of which their own colleagues strongly disapproved, and had given them a perma- nent lesson in 'parliamentary manners, as he was abundantly able to do, it would have been a good thing. No task of such extreme difficulty and deli- cacy has fallen to a Liberal leader in our time,as that with which he has had to cope since leading , his party in the House of Commons. And it is not surprising, therefore, that the rank and file cherish a sincere admiration and cordial apprecia- tion of the stolid pluck and straightforward devotion to the work of the pariy which has marked his leader- ship. It would bp easy., if this were the place, to show how he has been misunderstood by both friend and enemy—in the latter case, sometimes with inten- tion. The Liberal man in 'the street does not always understand how serious ffiie-drawn differences may be, but he has a keen recognition of the actual hewer of ' wood and drawer of water. In his performance of these hard and dull tasks for his party and his prin- ciples, Sir Henry Campbell-Bannerman may see the root of the feelings which never fail to greet him on the platfonn. —Ma. HENRY NORMAN, M.P., in "The World's Work for January.

THE YULE-LOG OF SORROW

(co )irigAt.)

BY FERGUS HUME,

Author of "Mystery of a Ransom Cab," "Hagar of the Pawn Shop," "The Clock Struck One," "The Rainbow Feather," &c. &c.

"It is a white Christmas of the traditional kind," said the man. ,

"And therefore should be a happy one, observed the girl. "Remember the old rhyme, Cyril: " 'A Yuletide green, Brings grief, I ween ; A Yuletide white, Doth bring delight.'"

Cyril Rixton shrugged his shoulders. "The rhyme speaks false, then," said he. "No amount of white could ever bring delight into this howling wilderness, let alone a happy Christmas." _

They were standing beneath an oak of mighty girth, which spread wide its leafless boughs. It was the only tree of any size amid that waste of snow.

In Rixton Park the woods had long since been levelled to the ground, and around the only surviving tree stretched a dismal expanse of ragge4 stumps half-buried in the 'freshly fallen snow. To the right, on the rise of the hill, stood the Tudor Grange, which had sheltered so many generations of Rixtons, but hereafter was to do so no more.

Cyril had every cause to feel aggrieved as he gazed upon this dilapidated demesne; for, in truth, it was more like an American backwood clearing than an English Park. The timber had been cut down and sold to pay interest on the mortgages with which the lands and Grange had been charged. There had been no other way of settling the debts, which had been allowed to accumulate for years. And even then it had been in vain.

What Regency Rixton had begun Victorian Rixton had completed. And now it had come that with the New Year the estate was to pass into the hands of strangers. Since the Wars of the Roses the family had dwelt in the Grange, but the hour had now struck and the doom was upon them, and the heir who was no heir looked his last on his lost inheritance. it was a bitter moment for "him, and even Hilda Markham could not consols

Yet her vrcsence, and symrathy should have been some consolation, for she was his affianced wife. When young Rixtrai had retutned from Oxford, two years before, he had found a new inmate at the Vicarage, none other tlyan Mr. Markham's niece, and with her he had straight way fallen in love. Ile did not know then that he would have no home to offer her. She was a clever student at Girton, he a graduate of Baliol. She loved him sufficiently well to engac,'e herself to him. She was studious, accomplished. and eminently feminine ‘vithal. He was no athlelic hearty young English- man, and one of the best. So they suited one another well enough, and in due season became engaged in the sober and sensible fashion of their unromantic kind. They were neither passionate ncr sentimental, but they loved and trusted one another with hear and soul. • And had things but core w Ii them as they should have done they wolfd have been a truly .happy couple—sedate and comfortable, making no noise in the world, but leading the drowsy. respecta!,le life which became them as magnates of their small sphere. Alas, it was not. to be ! Fate knocked at their door with a message of parting. Now they were spending their last Christmas together in the old home. "The last Christmas!" sighed Hilda with a shiver.

"The last ,for a time, dearest. Cheer up! The sun will shine again alt bough he is now behind a cloud, and a precious black one. There is still a fortnight till I leave, and you must not forget that it is to Africa I go, which is the modern Ophir. Who knows but that I ehAl make my fortune there, and return to buy back the estate." "I hope so, oh, I do hope so, Cyril. But can you 1 catty beer leaving your father?" "If I can leave you I can leave him. Besides, he will ho ma° table enough in his own way. Front the wreck of our f, etiMl there will be about three hundred a year for him, and that will be enough in the village down there." " Enou4ll! Oh, Cyril, think what he has been accustomed to."

Cyril sighed in his turn. ".,rue, true. But you know the saying, Hilda: You can't eat •your pie and have it. If any father had bert more careful in his youth he would not have to turn out of doors in his old age. Yet I suppose he is not altogether to blame. My great-grandfather started/ us downhill in George tV.'s time, and now we have got to the bottom at last. Nothing remains but to burn the Yule-log of Sorrow, and to clear out."

"The Yule-log of Sorrow?" "Here it is!" Cyril rapped the tree trunk with h;s stick. "Don't you know the story?" "No," replied Hilda, curiously. "Tell it to me. I love stories. The Yule-log of Sorrow? How strange. What does it mean?" "It means that this old tree will be cut down in a few days so that its trunk may blaze in the Grange hall as the funeral pyre of all the Rixtons. There is an old rhyme about it too. Come up to Ow house, Hilda, and thew it to you. I can tell you the story on the way." "It is strange that I have never heard-of this tradition."

" "Oh I don't know," replied Ilixton, bitterly. "We have had so many troubles to mourn over that there has been no opportunity for thinking or talking of much else. But now that. Sir Philip's prophecy is about to be fnitilled I expect the whole countryside will recall the legend." "A prophecy, yon say ? " "Well, a kind of prophe,ey. It's in doggerel rhyme, as such rubbish' should be. Sir Philip was certainly mo poet." "Who was he?" "A jaeobean ancestor of mine, who lived in the riga of the second James. He had a fancy for that oak yonder. Have you never wondered, Hilda, why of all the trees in the park that one was left anding ? " "Yes ; sometimes it has struck me as odd. but I havti never thought of asking the reason. Tell me al out it.

_ They were walking up what had once been the avenue, SUI tho crisp snow .crackled under their feet.. Cyril tucked Hilda's arm under his own and told her the legend. She listened attentively. An idle tale for an idle hour; so they thought it, yet the result of the telling surprised them both in after days._.. _ . .

"In r Philip's time that oak was alt-Tady of a goodly size," began Cyril, "and there was nothing about it to distinguish it from any other tree in the parlt. It gained its' frone,as the gallows-tree of Sir Philip's son. Young Walter Rixton fought for llontuout It at Sedgemoor, and, taken prisoner there, he was tried and condemned by Judge Jeffries at the Bloody Assizes. Never- the!ess his life was spared for a time, for King James wanted money. and Jeffries offered to pardon the rebel if his father would give fifty thousand pounds to-the King." "Fifty thousand pounds! Was Sir Philip so rich, !hen?" "He was and he was not. It seems that his e-randfather had gone to Peru, and while there bad found a buried treasure of the Incas consisting of je‘‘els. It was reported that he brought this home with hint, end the knowledge of it came to the ears of Charles I. Ile also wanted money. He tried to get the treasure from Sir Philip's grand- father; but the old man swore that the whole st,- - on ruin no

===== 0002601_19021226_art0111.txt | included=true | included =====
11. tried the same game wan icir rump ••-•*tsell, and offered to spare Walter's life, as I But in his turn Sir Philip denied the ~•01tence of the jewels. So Jeffries had his son langed on yonder oak." "Alow terrible," said Hilda. "But there could not have been any jewels, Cyril. or surely Sir Phiiip would have saved his son's hip."

"I agree with you, especially as in none of our family papers can we find a trace of this Inca treasure. At all events, Walter Rixton was hanged within sight of The Grange, and Sir Philip died a few months later, broken-hearted at his loss, 'Then the second son inherited the estate, and Iva are descended from him." "But what about the oak?"

"Oh, Sir Philip left a will in which he ordered that the oak upon which Walter had been hanged should not be cut down until such time as bad fortune turned the Riztons out of The Grange. Then it was to be levelled, and its trunk brought into the great hall, as the last yule-log of the family, to be burnt at the last Christmas festival. When all the trees were cut down to pay our debts, the Walter oak alone was spared; but now that the end has come, my father intends to use it as the yule-log this Christmas." "The Yule-log of Sorrow!" "Yes, it may well. be called that, for there is ”of-hintt but sorrow befere the Rixtons. Wait in the hall, Hilda, and I'll fetch you the rhyme I spoke of. It will interest you." In the hall, with its lofty oaken roof and sombre stained glass windows, the girl found Cyril's father with her uncle. They were seated by the ,reat hearth.

"You must be frozen to death, my dear," said Sir Graham, leadins: her to his own seat. "Warm ?ourself at the fire." "Nothing like wood for a royal blaze," said Ml'. eittrkhani, "but. one needs a fireplace like this to rake it in, eh, Rizton ?"

. . "The royal blaze is yet to come, Markham, on aristinas Eve." "Ah, you are talking of the Yule-log of Sorrow, ir Graham," said Hilda, warming her pretty +Aids. "Cyril has just been telling me about it. He's gone to find the prophecy for me to see." "It is not a prophecy," replied the baronet, testily, "only some wretched doggerel, meaning nothing that I can see. Sir Philip lost his reason titer the death of his son, I fancy." "Small blame to him," grumbled the vicar, rubbing his thin legs, "how ivould you like to see ''.."`dyril hanged at your own door, Rixtun ? " "Oh uncle don't!"

"I am afraid no more Rixtons will be hanged at 'heir own door," said Sir Graham, grimly. "They won't have a door to be hanged at—no, nor a tree to c4an4le from. Walter's oak is the ltsst in the pirk. and I'll have it cut down this week, and we'll burn it as a yule-log on this very hearth." "Are you going to keep Christmas, then, in the Ad style ?" a-ked Hilda. „ —•

"Certainly, my dear. With such little money as remains I intend to celebrate the good old customs. The whole village shall come. There shall be dances, games, mummers, and the yule- log —all exactly as Sir Philip desired in his will." "But, my dear Rixton, this is surely no time for revelry," prot est ed the vicar. "It is the titiv,,," insisted Sir Graham, with an imperious nod. "Before The Grange passes from me and mine, I shall keep one last Christmas as my ancestors did. There will be no more revels in this hall when lam gone. The new generation thinks nothing of the season and its associations. No, Markham ; since the end has come, let me depart with dignity. Your church bells shall ring We out and I shall hold a Belshazzar's feast."

"rThe hand-writing has been on the wall for some Mine," muttered Markham. But Sir Graham did not hear him. He might have protested if he had. But his attention was taken up by his on entering at that moment with a paper in his hand. "Here you are. Hilda," cried Cyril, coming towards her. "I've copied out the rhyme, and modernised the spelling. You can take this with you, and see if you can make out. its riddle." The girl spread out the paper on the table near the light, and read out the rhyme as Cyril had written it:

"When 111-luck Names For Rixton's Oak, Mad Loss Of Grange And Lend; In Grimmest Hour Take Glee, Evoke Much Strength Till Oblation's Made—And Keep Embers Red In Grange—Have Trust."

Twice she repeated it. She looked round in perplexity. Cyril laughed and shook his head, as if to intimate that he could not explain. But Sir Graham came to the rescue.

"It just means that when we Rixtons leave The Grange, we must cut down the <>A, and, after' burning it, as a yule-log, keep the ashes; for what purpose, I cannot tell, nor could Sir Philip him- self, I suspect. . The old nian wrote it when he was crazed with grief_ for the loss of his son." "Is this a facsimile of the original, Cyril? "Yes, and no. 1 have modernised the spelling, but each word begins' with a ,capitdd letter, as you see, and the metre is quite irre.zuh.r." "Yes, somewhat," said the vicar, musing over the verse. "It is sad doggerel. What it means I cannot say; yet the last lines would seem to intimate that something was to be expected from the previous ones." "Well, well," said Sir Graham, "the oak shall be cut down and burnt. Then we shall see what will be the result. But I hope for no miracle." "In the meantime Hilda can see what she can do towards a rending of the riddle," Erad Cyril. "Well, I shall certainly try," replied the girl; "it is quite possible there may be more in it, than meets the eye.'; "So many have thought, my dear, but no one has made much of it so far. You're not going, Markham?"

"Yes, thanks, I think I ioust.' I have my Christmas sermon to prepare. ana it's getting late." Left alone, Cyril and his father sat looking into the fire. The one thought was uppermost in both their minds. At last Sir Graham broke the silence.

"Yon are getting everything in readiness for Christmas Eve, I hope, my boy? " "Yes, father, it's all arranged for. They are delighted at the idea of the mumming." "Well, they are dancing over our graves," said the old man, bitterly. "It is hard that for the sins of my forefathers I should have to lose all I have in the world—position, home, even my son." "You lose tne only for a time, father. I hope to do well in Africa. Who knows, I might even make my fortune there."

That week there was great excitement in the village of Hazelwood. All energies where given over to the preparations for the festival at The Grange. The parish clerk, himself taking the part of St. George, instructed the mummers how to play their several iaes. Hilda busied herself with decorating the church and teaching carols to the choir. Occasionally she would t,ike from her pocket the rhyme of Sir Philip and look at it, hut each time a became more inscrutable to her. She could

make nothing of it. She was forced to 4onclude that it was merely a repetition in rude verse of the ' order already contained in Sir Philip's will that the Walter oak should be converted into Christmas fuel. In no other way could she construe it. The doom which threatened must fall ; and this so- called prophecy, far front tending to avert it, seemed but a mere mockery of the inevitable. All the idle fellows in the village assembled to have a chop at the great tree, and here, also, there came withered old folk to lament that the glory had departed from the house of Rixton. Many were the sympathising looks cast on Sir Graham, but none dared to offer him sympathy, or attempted to hold out a hope that matters would mend. With the falling of the oak the end was come, the doom was accomplished, and as the strokes rang and the white chips flew many a grandame muttered the rhyme of Sir Philin

lEnvels. 131 r MIT refused the Ring's offer, and Walter Riaton was hange-.1 on the oak tree, which is now burning on the hearth." "My dear," remonstrated her uncle, "we have heard all this. It is a twice-told tale." "It is one that has never been ended, and I intend to finish it now," said Hilda. quietly. "I do not know if my ending is a true one, since I have invented it only on the slightest of evidence. Still I think it is, as you shall judge. On the death of his son," she continued, "Sir Philip, api ra'iensive that the jewels would be violently seized by James, determined to conceal them. He chose the trunk of the oak for the purpose." Here the eyes of all were fixed on the yule-log. "Yes, in that very trunk he cut a hole, and placed the jewels in it, filling it up afterwards. Then, as he died before his second son returned, he did not dare to tell anyone what he had done, lest the jewels should be stolen. He, therefore, left a clue to the place in a rhyme; and in his will he stated that when the Rixtons were in danger of losing the estates, the oak should be cut down and burnt as a yule-log, when the jewels would be discovered in the ashes."

"Hilda!" cried Sir Graham, much agitated, "this cannot be true. Your story is not credible." "All the same, Sir Graham, I believe it is. I thought that a cryptogram was concealed in those foolish rhymes, and have been all the week trying to find the key. To-day I did find it, as I will chew you. But to prove its truth, order yonder log to be split, and no doubt you will find the jewels within—that is, if my belief is a true one." "We will try at once," cried Cyril, jumping up. "Here! Got crowbars and wedges. The log is cracked and charred, so it will not be difficult to rive it asunder.. Quick! quick!" At least a dozen were eager to carry out his command and speedily returned with wedges and hammers. The wedges were placed in the crack which extended down the log, and the hammers were wielded with right good will. In the mean- time the rest of the company, headed by Sir Graham and his son, crowded round Hilda at the table, greatly excited and anxious for her explana- tion. Hilda produced the rhyme which Cyril had written out, and a paper on which was some writ- ing of her own. "It is very simple," said she. "You take the initial letters of each word, and put them together. I guessed this because every word commenced in the most unusual manner with a 'capital letter. Take the first and second lines and half the third: When 111-luck Names For Rixton's Oak' Mad Loss Of Grange And Lands, In Grimmest Hour Take—' Now take the initial letter, Cyril, and see the sentences they make." Cyril did so, writing them down with a hand shaking from excitement, and read out: "They make a line:

===== 0002601_19021226_art0114.txt | included=true | included =====
Win, from log alight—' "

"That is correct," said Hilda, taking up her own writing. "The whole of the secret terse can be worked out in the same way. It's plain enough. Listen." And she read:

Win, from log alight, Gems to make right.' "

Hardly had the last word dropped from her mouth when a loud cracking was heard, which drew everyone round the fire. Parted by the wedges, and charred beneath by the fierce fire which was eating into its heart, the log split asunder with a flurry of sparks; and a volley of dames shot into the air. Cyril, who was bending over the split log, uttered a cry, as he peered down- ward through the smoke and flame. In the red heart of the ashes appeared a small box apparently of iron or steel, which glowed red-hot where the fire had burnt it underneath.

"It is true—true. I see a box. Here, quick! Bring a bucket of water. Father, give me th,a in a few minutes these directions were obeyed, and out of the fire's heart Cyril, pale with excite- ment, lifted with tongs and shovel the still glowing box. It was no easy job, but by aid of billets of wood picked up it random, the box was dropped into • a- large bucket of ice-cold water. It disappeared with a sharp, hissing sound, and a cloud of white steam rolled up. While the box was cooling, Sir Graham sent the servants for chisels and wrenches to force it open. "Good God!" he cried, greatly agitated, "if it should contain the jew els after we should be eavtd! Here, Cyril, here on the table." Cyril, by this time, judging that the box was Cool enough, had tilted over the bucket, now steaming and hot. The box fell on the floor, and testing it with his finger, he found it was sufficiently eciolecl to pick it up. In ZL moment it was on the tsble, and the village blacksmith was prizing up the lid. The box was simply locked, and not soldered in any way. When forced, the lid flew open so suddenly that the casket itself escaped out of the man's hands, and the contents rattled out on the oaken table. Then a cheer arose, and Sir Graham gave a gasp of wonder, for out of it rolled diamonds, emeralds, turquoise stones, and the most wonderful of opals. The mass of gems flashed rainbow hues in the light, and lay there before the astonished eyes of the company— proof positive of Hilda's correct interpretation of the rhyme. A king's ransom sparkled before them. "Fifty thousand pounds!" gasped Sir -Graham. "0 my son! 0 Hilda!".

He °was so overcome that he sank back fainting in the chair, and Cyril hastily brought a glass of brandy to revive him. "I was right, I was right," cried Hilda, clapping her hands. "0 Cyril! Cyril!" "My good angel " And clasping her in his arms he kissed her before them all. Then there arose such a cheer as had not been heard in the old halt for many a lone. day. The men shouted, the women wept, many shook hands, and all congratulated Sir Graham, who, on his part. was too nmeh agitated to reply. And in the fireplace the yule-log, having yielded up the secret it had so wonderfully preserved for two hundred years, flared and flamed in a glorious blaze. "The Yule-log of'Joyl " cried Cyril. "God bless it, and you, my own Hilda." When quiet was restored the jewels' after a thorough examination, were replaced in the casket, and the prophecy with its secret couplet, which Hilda had so cleverly discovered, was re-read and discussed. Mr. Markham, in his character of vicar, delivered a short sermon on the strength of it.

"Observe," said he, "the last two words are: Have trust,' which shows that Sir Philip Ilixton guessed that in their despair his descendants might lose their trust in God. Fortunately he was wrong, for Sir Graham and his son have always confided their cares to the Almighty. Now their belief in His goodness has been rewarded, and they can still dwell under their own fig-tree." "Hurrah!" cried Cyril, joyously. "I shall not need to co to Africa now, and we can pay off all the mortgages." "Thank God!" said Sir Graham, reverently, "and you • will bring home to the old Grange the good fairy to whom we owe this wonderful dis- covery. Hilda, my dear, kiss me. You have saved Cyril and myself, and next year we shall be able to hold another Christmas festivity in our own hall. Thank God once more for His great mercy." "That we must do now," said the vicar, looking around. "Let us adopt Sir Philip's advice, and have trust in God's justice and mercy. Let us pray." And then and there the whole company, rich and poor, gentle and simple, knelt down while Mr. Markham offered up a beautiful and affecting thanksgiving to God for having so dealt with His servant as to save him in his hour of need. Hardly had he ended Nvhen far away sounded a joyful Deal 1-11.

"Christmas! Christmas! " shouted Cyril, and taking Hilda by the hand he ran out on the terrace followed by the company. The snows far and wide sparkled in the moon- light, and in the village below the bells rang out joyously from Hazelwood Church, proclaiming the birth of Christ. Hand in hand the lovers stood on the terrace with Sir Graham and the vicar beside them. Every heart was too full of joy and thankfulness to speak, but there was no need, for from below there suddenly sounded the clear voices of boys singing the Christmas carol. And the bells rang, and the clear voices floated through the frosty air, and the yule-log—no longer of sorrow, but of joy—flamed in the hall. Cyril kissed Hilda, and the voices again sang their message of gladness.

Madame Christian gave the solo "Divine Redeemer," in an effective manner, and Mr. Dawson, L.V.C.M., officiated at the organ. Messrs Balmford and Few took part in the service, and the Pastor gave a short address on the origin of the Christian Festival.

NORTHERN RUGBY UNION. THE LEAGUE.---FIRST DIVISION.

WARRINGTON v. BRADFORD.

There was a crowd of about 6,000 on the Warrington ground last Saturday, when Bradford were the visitors, but the joyful anticipations of many were dashed to the ground when it was learnt that both Isherwood and Fish were absentees, the former through injury,and the latter through failure to comply with the working clause., Bradford had a strong side, Marsden being the only absentee, and his place was filled by Broadjey. Brooks and Knight found places in the home three-quarter line. A warm •Nalcome was accorded Dickenson and Hallam when they stepped on the field. Mr. Farrar, of Halifax, was the referee, and the teams were as follows : Waftington:—Hallam, back; Brookes, Knight, Dick- enson, and Harris, three-quarter backs; Burgess and Hockenhall, half backs; Boardman, Taylor, Naylor, Harmer,Bdmondson, Morrison, Coleman, and F. Swift, forwards. _

Bradford :—Ward, back; Dunbavin, Mosby, Eagers, and Sinton, three-quarter backs • Broadley and Foster, half backs; Cheardley, Smales iurner,Laidlaw,Sharatt, Carroll, Feathers, and Dunn, forwards. Warrington kicked off with a stiff breeze against them, and early on Brookes became conflpicuous by preventing what seemed like a sure score. Warrington were penalised,and Mosby kicked a goal for the visitors. Fast play followed, and the ball was taken into the Bradford quarters. Broadley relieved, but smart work by Hockenhall again transferred play to the Bradford end, but the effort was spoiled by Brookes failing to catch. Harris stopped a promising movement by the Bradford three-quarters, and after a penalty had fallen to Warringtonjlagers gained possession, and scored a ' doubtful try for Bradford, also adding the extra points. Half-time score—Bradford 2 goals, 1 try (7 points), Warrington nil. On restar ting,plar was,for a time, con- ' fined to the centre. Burgess initiated a good passing movement, but the Warrington men were stopped at the corner. Bradford then had a turn and looked danger- ous. Exciting play was witnessed on the line, but Burgess relieved slightly by a kick. The ball soon came back, and several scrummages tookplace. Dicken- son made a brilliant effort, but was pulled up a few yards from the line. Warrington kept up the pressure and Burgess scored a try which was disallowed. Towards the close, Sinton gained possession, and scored at the corner. The goal-kick failed. Time arrived with the score :—Bradford 2 goals, 2 tries (10 points), Warring- ton nil.

Not immediately above the sea, but a mile above Imo-level, away among the less-visited Alps of the far east of Switzerland, is one of the most interesting little towns in Europe. Its name is Davos, and it is the most important place on the Continent, if not in the world, that is devoted principally to affording facilities For carrying out the mountain -cure for consumption. It is also largely frequented in winter on account of the unparalelled opportunities it offers for skating, tobogganing and other similar sports, while in summer It is a tolerably busy centre of tourist activity. These things produce a pleasant stir and bustle in its streets, but the fact still remains that a large proportion of its. visitors are invalids, who are quietly but effectively recovering from consumption, in one of the most de- ightful climates in the world.

winter weather. 'The last-named feature, in con- junction with the warmth of the sun and the dryness of the air (which lessens the sensation of cold),enables one to spend a very great proportion of one's time in the open air, some patients even sleeping out of doors summer and winter. Generally speaking the most important effect of the mountain climate on the human body is to invigorate and strengthen it. It acts most powerfully (and one might almost say specifically) in diseases of the lungs and nerves. One of the first noticed effects is generally the development of an immense appetite and the rapid disappearance of most forms of indigestion. The greatest luxury the

===== 0002601_19021226_art0144.txt | included=true | included =====
The conditions of life at an elevation of 5,000 feet above the level of the sea, naturally differ a great deal from those of the lowlands. The first difference one notices is that in temperature. For nearly half the vear the valley in which Davos lies is under snow. Yet, although the thermometer not infrequently goes down to the neighbourhood of Fero,nowhere in Europe, not even on the Riviera oramong the Isles,of Greeee, does one enjoy so much solar heat in winter as here among the snow and ice of the high mountains. This is a result, chiefly, of the extreme rarity of the air. The sun's rays have an intensity, ei an in the height of winter, that is seldom felt in England in the warmest parts of summer. Those who have never been a mile above sea-level during the winter cannot possibly realise the meteorological condition of things. At one

)1 Ls

===== 0002601_19021226_art0145.txt | included=true | included =====
climate affords is the fore-mentioned sun-bath among the snow. No amount of description could do justice to it. It must be experienced to be realised. But it is not alone • for those in search of health that the DAvos of winter offers its attractions. It is a magnificent pl4ground for those who have the good fortune to be in full health andY have the inclination and the opportunity of exchanging the fog and gloom of an English winter for a few weeks of warm sunshine and the invigorating air of the moun- tains. For nearly a hundred days the skater may revel in the well-appointed rinks, while toboganning, a sport almost unknown in England, runs it -

- -.h.--

•

===== 0002601_19021226_art0146.txt | included=true | included =====
extremity jof the:body one wears snowboots and at the other a straw hat. While the whole valley is perhaps four feet deep in snow, the heat of the sun is so great that the invalid, when he lies out to bask on his balcony chair' often has to protect his bead and chest by blinds from the • powerful rays of the sun. The thermometer at his side may register 110 degrees in the sun, while yonder a gay international crowd is enjoying itself on the skating Rink. It is the actual sun-rays that are so hot; the air remains always cool, so that while the invalid is basking in his 110 degrees the skaters feel no more discomfort from the heat than they would on a winter'sl day in England. Another strange feature of the climate is that, in spite of the great contrasts in temperature, no one islany the worse for the sudden and fregilent

second in the affection and attentions of the sporting winter yisitor. Ski-lug, curling, ins-hockey and other winter sports offer in their turn attractions to those who can spare time from 'skating and tobogganing. Although the climate is at its bast in winter, and, considering the drawbacks of that season in the United Kingdom, is then most valu'ible, it is none the less pleasant and healthy in summer. The high-mountain 'meadows in Jtme are almost as unbelievable to those who have not seen them, as is the wonderful heat of the sun during the snows of winter. The lusciousness of the growth and the infinite variety of the flowers lectly marvellous, In the Davos Valley alone

changes, and, in fact, nowhere in the world is one so free from that 'coinmon affliction, a" cold." The chief cause of this is the extreme dryness of the. air. The physical changes wx mght in the Helen organ- ism by the high mountain climate are most interesting and important, though it would lead us too far into medical science to deal with them here. It must suffice to mention that the features of the climate that are most importantsfrom the point of view of health are: (1) the extreme purity of the air (freedom from bacteria; and other floating matter); (2) cbraing low temperatures accompanied by (3) abundant and very powerful sunshine; (4) the rarity of the air and (5) its dryness, both .of which' atmospheric qualities have a beneficial effect on, the lungs ; 'andj6) the preralence of calm in the air during fine

the flora comprises nearly thousand species. At all ,times of the year,moreover, one is a urrouuded by the limpressive splendours of the high-mountain scenery, and the, almost theatrical splendour of sunrise and sun- set on the snowy peaks defies the artist to reproduce them, and tinges the life of the remote and isolated !valley with a kind of spiritual beauty. Life a !mile above the sea has an infinitude of enjoyments for all 'win can appreciate the luxary of basking •in the sun land can appreciate the beauty of form and colour that are to be seen in the thin pare air so high above the customary haunts of men.

LETTERS TO THE EDITOR.

-NOTICE TO CORRESPONDENTS.

Authors of letters intended for publication must Mall cases send their names and addresses to the Editor as a guarantee of good faith. Matter intended for publication must be written on one side only of the paper and sent in, if possible,xiot I later than Thursday.

Correspondents tire strongly advised to write briefly. A short letter has a much greater chance of insertion than a long one. We cannot under ordinary circumstances undertake to return the 'manuscripts which are not used.
