For the Ancient Seamen.

XMAS, 1902.

Dear Sir,—PerMit me through the medium of your columns to ask on behalf of poor aged worn-out British seamen, for the urgently needed help of your numerous readers.

There are hundreds of "England's ancient Mariners" who will have to faoe an almost fireless grate and a scanty cupboard this C'-ristmas season, and the Royal Alfred Aged Merchant Seamen's Institution has hoisted the signal of distress on behalf of these poor old tars, and anxiously await the response.§ The "Royal Alfred" is the National and Unique Haven foraged and destitute British Seamen, and 1,800 deserving old sailors have been permanently relieved during their last few years on earth, either by a small pension or a comfortable home. We want to throw out the life-line to these old sailors shipwrecked on Life's Ocean, and we plead for help.— Yours faithfully, J. BAILEY WALKER, Secretary. Royal Alfred Aged Merchant Seamen's Institution, 58, Fenchurch-street, E.C. -- 1 The Recent Eisteddfod.

Deer Sir,—As ray name has been dragged into the con- troversy about the recent Eisteddfod a. Warrington, I trust you will extend your courtesy, and allow nie to reply. First of all, Mr. Payton speaks of choirshaving any assistance, as wrong in principle. According to his 'high code of morals it may be, but in this case the five men complained of are bonaflde members or both Runcorn and St.Helens, registered on the books of each society, and duly attend rehearsal when required. What is wrong in such proceeding? Take cricket for instance. Almost every Saturday, during the cricket season, teams go to play matches with perhaps 9 or 10 men. When they arrive at the ground, an effort is made to complete the team;a 11 the better if the substitutes can play the gains. Now what follows? If the team who come two men short, win, does the defeated side whimper and wkine, and complain of theassistance given by the subs.? No Sir, they act like men, shake hands and express the hope for better luck, next time the teams may meet., Then Mr. Payton goes on to make the most scandab ous assertion it has ever been my lot to meet. He says— "granted Mr. Berrey was entitled to conduct the Runcorn choir, still considering he had a great deal to do with promoting the E;steddfod, it was bad form for him to take any active part in the competi- tion, and I have reason to believe that it was a mere blind to put Mr, Brown's name as conductor of the Runcorn choir." • •

Now, sir, these lines are a gross libel upon me, and I swear that in no shape or form whatever, had I any- thing to do with the promotion of the competition. I held aloof, and took no more part in the affair than ?qr. Payton himself. (I might have done and had the offer, but that is another tale). How any man whose sense of right and wrong is so beautifully perfect, could sioop to write such mean, contemptible words, passesniy com- prehension altogether. I have heard Mr. Payton spoken of as a very nice gentleman, and sincerely hope he is man enough to admit having done me a very grievous wrong. In fact I demand suchapology, although I hope his high sense of what is becoming of a nice gentleman, will lead him to play the man without any further reminder.

The Warrington Apollo Choir are quite capable of replying to all the insinuations re the management of I the competition, and doubtless Mr. Payton and his friends will hear further from them. Such vile inuendoes are not worthy of those who wrote them, and the Christian charity they pretend to uphold, ought to show itself in their dealing with their fellow men. Mr. Payton must be very inexperienced in musical competitions, or he would know that a choir or band entering into competition, can change its constitution or select another conductor, whether published or not, at any time before the competition. This is an unwritten law throughout the world—unless specially provided by rules issued by the promoters. "Competi- tions open to all the world" is a very general rule printed in the syllabus. Mr. Payton says he would like an instance when a given conauctor :ionducted more than one band at any competition. If Mr. Payton will take the trouble to enqnire from any bandsman n Warrington, ho willbetold that at almost every cosupetition there are conductors engaged to eenduct more than one band. I have before me as I write, five programmes of com- petitions at Belle Vue and the Crystal Palace, in one case the same conductor: conducted five bands, in another case four bands, in five other cases two bands each. Now Mr. Payton where are you? Sir,it would have been perfectly legitimate for Mr. Payton to have con- ducted all the choirs at the recent Eisteddfod, if the choirs could trust him to do his best for all.

The other letters being signed anonymously are scarcely worth one dip of the ink. If a man has right on his side, why hide under a nom de plume? It is only 1 cowards who do this. There is one statement I must reply to, as it concerns the Runcorn Choir., "Interesced" says (by the way what a beautiful busybody f he must be) "he had made in quiries among the members of the Runcorn Choir and he is assured that Runcorn have no St. Helens men on their books and are not members of the choir." Dear me, Row we'll see what sort of an Axmaias ourfriend is. Last night at the Runcorn rehearsal I put this question to every Runcorn man "has anyone from Warrington made any inquiries re the status of members of this Zhoir, or any membier who sang at Warrington" and in every case 1 the answir was" No." Now Mr. Interested we shall all vote you be awarded the highest place as" Champion per- verter of the truth." The men complained of are members of the Runcorn Choir and the St. Helens Choir. Surely a man can be a member of more than one choir, if he is worthy a place. Mr. Payton has members in his Male Voice Choir, who are members of three district War- rington Choirs, so it is not very difficult to be members of ,two.

Now sir, Mr. Payton says he is not afraid of me, I don't And a line anywhere to that effect, butt 9hallenge him and his same choir, to a contest at Warrington, Runcorn to have only bona-fide residents, for a prize of £lO or upwards, judge to be mutually agreed upon and sing the same piece. This offer remains open until the 31st day of December, 1902, the loser to be awarded a carved wooden spoon, as at the University. I hare conducted in 79 competitions up to date, and have never heard or seen such unsportsmanlike feeling shown by one choir, against another. If Mr. Payton will go to Wales, the home of Eisteddfods he will find at almost every competition the very officials competing in the choirs and orchestras (not in solos). The men don't hide it, they come on the platform, with the official rosette, this is done even at the National Eisteddfod,and yet no one grumbles, why should they? . Everyone has faith in the judge, and if you have not, why compete? Reside Runcorn and St. Helens, Mr. Payton objects to Makerfleld and to Wigan for allowing their singers to assist. Now sir, it is perfectly plain,Mr. Payton would' like no other choir to compete, and then, he might win. Sir, at any competition, I have ever taken part in, I defy any • man to prove that I have ever acted in any other manner than that of an honest man, and when I have been defeated (and am not at all ashamed to Say I have many times) I congratulate the winner and neverresort to the kicking business. If any objection is warranted,everyone is given a full chance to send a protest, before the award. It is mean to fly to the papers and try and ex- plain how it is we don't win. As a rule when we don't win, we don't deserve the prize. have been invited by Warrington and Runcorn friends (and I flatter myself they are many), to do my best for them. The result of my efforts T am content to leave the public to judge, and T assure Mr. Payton and his friends, that I shall continue to do my duty, in face of the most bitter opposition. We are all sending the splendid motto abroad just now "Peace on garth good will to men," and from my heart I say, Amen. I am willing to meet Mr. Payton any time and be friends. Thanking you air, in anticipation of your kindness. Yours sincerely,

H. BERRHY. Rtndon.Terrace, St. Helens, Dec., 23rd.

CHRISTMASTIDE. The Festivities at Warrington.

Christians the.season of joy and goodwill, has once more come and gone. The weather which is identi- fied with the "old-fashioned" Christmas, has again been lacking. King Frost has held entirely aloof, in fact; the mildness of the atmosphere has been more characteristic of early spring than of . mid-winter. If the climatic conditions were not typical, there were, however, 'during the week indications on every side that the great festival was at • hand. Tradesmen had vied with one another in making their windows look ,as attractive as\ possible. At 801110 shops Christmas fare was alluringly displayed, whilst at others there were toys and a variety of wares with ! which "Santa Claus' could make glad the,hearts of , the youngsters who so anxiously awaited his visit. The butchers, as usual, had a splendid show, the car- cases of numerous prize beasts being on sale.. Unfor- tunately trade does not seem to be at all brisk at pre- sent, and if the weather had been severe distress would ' no doubt have been accentuated. As .it was, outdoor employments were not interfered With, and those who long for "old-fashioned Christmases " would do well to bear this side of the question in mind. The old custom of carol-:singing has of late years degenerated somewhat. This year householders have for weeks before Christi:ins, been subjected to continual annoy- ance by the numerous bands of youtigsters, who. in- dulge in the singing of " carols " of the Christmas is coming and the 'goose is getting fat" type, which make one feel inclined to resort to stern measures to secure peace and quietness. To real carol-singing, however, no one can have any abjection, and several choirs have acquitted. themselves well in this respect. 'there has also beet 'a great deal of instrumental music —both appropriate and inappropriate.

===== 0002601_19021226_art0150.txt | included=true | included =====
AT THE BARRACKS.

That "Tommy Atkins" has an eye for the artistiO side of things was evident from the elaborate Christ- mas decorations at Orford Barracks. The soldiers had spared no pains to make the usually dull, bare rooms as attractive as possible and the result of their handiwork was most creditable to them. The room allotted to the festivities of the South Lancashire Regiment had re- ceived the most attention. Over the entrance was a I motto bidding visitors a hearty welcome. At the far end of the room were the arms of the regiment, together with a list of honours won. A profusion of evergreens and various-coloured papers had been utilized for decora- tive purposes and altogether, the display Was very effective. On the walls were seasonable greetings to the officers. Two rooms were set apart for the festi- vities in Connection with the King's Liverpool Regiment. They had also been lavishly decorated, and a striking feature in one of them was a canvas sheet on which the regimental crest and colours, together With a list of honours achieved by the regiment, had been artistically painted by Drummer Huswick. On Christmas morning the soldiers attended church, and afterwards a sumptuous Christmas dinner was served. Some idea of the ample provision made for the "Tommies" in regard to edibles, may be gained when it is stated that for the men of each regiment there were no fewer than 12 turkeys, 12 geese, 12 shoulders of pork, and 12 boiled hams. Of course, there was the inevitable plum pudding. In the evening dancing was indulged in, and a very pleasant time was spent.

===== 0002601_19021226_art0151.txt | included=true | included =====
At Runcorn.

Christmastide at Runcorn was observed in pretty much the same manner as in preVious years. Tuesday evening was regarded as "show night," and there was a very large display of good Christmas fare, the butchers' and poulterers' shops looking especiallywell. On Wed- nesday night the exceedingly mild weather tempted a large number of people to parade the streets. There were many bands of singers carolling into the early morning the birth of Christmas Day. At the Parish Church and at most of the Nonconformist places of worship, special services were held in the morning, there being very large attendances. The holidays, on the whole, passed off very quietly indeed. Widnes.

The festive season was ushered in in the customary fashion in the chemical metropolis by the carolling of waits and universal hearty greetings. Work was, of course, suspended at the chemical and other manufac- tories, and the docks and canals were practically deserted. During the day services were held at the various churches and chapels. In the morn- ing the animal club handicap and championship of the Farnworth Harriers took pla 0vc..... m.ight .and ile coarse; on Widnes Football Club's ground, before a thousand spectators. A grand race resulted-4,1. Lea, 5 mins. 40 secs. start; 2, T. lace, 30 secs.; 3, P. Grady, 30 secs. J. Hosker, however, the most celebrated member of the club, completed the course in .the quickest time from scratch, and secured the championship add gold medal given by 'Colonel Hall- Walker, M.P. J. Wilkinson, 2mins, 25secs, won the novice's medal. Councillor Laws and Mr. J. C. Evans ably officiated as judges, and subsequently, at the club's head-quarters, Councillor Laws, the president, distributed the medals to,the successful competitors. Forty competed in the race. In the afternoon there was a large attendance at a Football match between Widnes A and Widnes Rangers and Widnes White Star—a match atmnged for the en- couragement of junior football in the borough. The A team won easily by 20 points to 7.

CHRISTMAS DAY FOOTf3ALL.

WARRINGTON V. LEIGH.

Warrington journeyed to Leigh on Christnias Day with a full team and high hopes of victory. There were no.trains so the team wen.t by 'bus and a good number of supporters followed. Leigh were also fully represented, and there was a fine gate of about 4,000 people. The weather was spoiled by a tremendous wind which blew down the field. Mr. Smirk of Wigan was the referee and the teams. were as follows.

Leigh: Pearson, full-back; Ganley, Bennett, Towers, and Neville, three-quarters; Johnson and Hinson, half- backs; Boardman (captain), W. and J. Roberts, Kelly, Jameson, Clare, Webster, and Kirk, forwards. Warrington: Hallam, full back; Fish, Isherwood, Dickenson and Harris, threequarters ; Burgess and Hockenhall, half-backs; Taylor, Naylor, Boardman. Morrison, Swift) Coleman, Harmer and Edmondson forwards. The wind was so strong that four periods of twenty minutes were played, Swift startini against the wind, and Johnson mulling the return. Then Neville made a short dash along touch, but was sent in. Leigh had a penalty, Burgess and Fish passing back to centre, where Warrington had a penalty which was of little use. A lon.' runt by Leigh saw Warrington in a pickle, Dicken- sonkicking in touchat the corner. Leigh attacked, but Johnson was held close in. A moment, later, however, he got hold and slipped over smartly with the first try for sewn weeks, and Leigh folk cheered lustily. Towers nilssed the goal. Warrington at last attacked, Hocken- hall breaking away cleverly, but his pass went wrong.: Warrington again got away. but Isherwood's pass to Fish was slightly forward. Leigh next got :mice penalty shot which Towers made nothing of. A moment later quarter time was called, with Warrington defending. Johnson ,restarted and Dickenson was collared before he could kick. Leigh got possession and passed across with no result. Warrington eventually go down *ith a penalty, the ball, however, going dead from Fish's kick. Leigh got the ball continually, but Isherwood at last found touch near the Leigh line. Burgess nearly dodged over from the scrimmage. Leigh cleared and Hinson and Bennett broke away, the latter giving Neville a shocking pass at centre and a good opening went beg- ging. Hockenhall broke away grandly and passed to Harris who was pushed in touch at the corner. Fisk kicked a penalty goal the next minute. Leigh attacked' vigorously and Warrington put in some very effective tackling. Burgess relieved, and Dickenson downed Pearson. Fish twice got hold, but the whistle went when he had his best chance. Then Burgess kicked a dead ball and the whistle went for the interval with Leigh leading by a try to it goal. The duel now commenced in earnest Leigh, coming down with Rile passing between Johnson and Bennett. A scrummage was formed in the corner, and Dickenson was forced over his own line. Boardman cleared from the syds scrummage but Leigh maintained shot attack, Fish intercepted and dodged his way to Pearson who got him by the feet. At last Warrington got to the I centre from a fine kick by Burgess. Leigh were soon hack and play was of the keenest description, War- rington defending very well. Just before the change Leigh got close up,a penalty helping Warrington. Thiere MIS no sgoring and Warrington crossed over a point' be- hind.

CHATTY PARAGRAPHS.

It is easy to understand the sympathetic interest which has been manifested by the King and Queen in• the illness of the Countess of Dudley. 'The energy with which the Earl and Countess have set themselves to understand all Irish problems, and particularly the con- dition of the people in all parts of ,the island, has been remarked on more than one occasion, but the King and Queen must have been particularly tcliched by the fact that, the illness of her Excellency is similar to that from which the King suffered a few months ago. it is a fact for which no explanation appears to have beendiscover- ed that men are much morejliable to appendicitis than women, but there have been several cases among people of high rank, which shew that the weaker sex are by no, means exempt from the attacks of this terrible disease, and there are, of course, a great many other instances among people whose sufferings are not chronicledin the newspapers.

Them:edding of Lady Sybil Primrose, which will take place early in the New Year, will be an event of great social interest, and inn sense, a great loss to her father. Lord Rosebery has always been devoted to his children, and must greatly feel the parting with them. His eldest son Lord bahnerry will come of age next year, and- since the marriage of her sister to the Earl of Crewe, Lady Sybil has been the constant companion of her father. She was recently one of the King's birthday party at Sandringham, and like Miss Dorothy Drew, she and her sister, were privately received by the late Queen Victoria, who took an affectionate interest in Lord Rosebery's danghters. Lady Sybil has been one of the, few girl hostesses in Society and it will be a great loss to Lord Rosebery when she relinquishes the task of pre- siding over her father's hospilatities. I The old hard and fast rule that a girl must not go anywhere until she has made her curtsy to her sovereign, has vanished. Instead she is allowed a preliminary frolic, during the previous winter, this being appropri- ately termed the "breaking in" process. Lady Marjorie Greville, the only daughter of Lord and Lady Warwick, whose coming-out ball quite recently took place at Baton Lodge, is attracting much attention just now, so, also is Miss Sybil Brodnck, Miss Diana Start, Miss Marjorie Coke, Miss Mary Manners, Miss Violet Bampfylde and Miss Lucy Beckett, all of whom are to. be presented in the spring. Frocks for the debutanteere not so easy as they appear to be. White, of course, is chosen, but not all even fresh complexions can wear this Unrelieved, and then there is , the fashioning, which invariably presents difficulties. Angularities have to be hidden, trimmings must not be over-done, the whole has to be girlish-looking, and fashionable. Lady Marjorie Greville's gown was of white tulle powdered with silver, a combination quite possible with her brilliant complexion. Very dainty debutante frocks are being prepared of white chiffon, tulle, silk, gauze, silk muslin, or point d'esprit net, over Japanese silk, the favourite form of trimming being frills, tuck- lags, gaugings, and puffins of the same fabric,and trails and knots of any "young " flower, such as violets,daises, or snoWdrops. The little Empire frocks for quite small girls are particularly fascinating. The skirts of Japanese silk, nun's veiling, or cashmere,—these materials being more suitable for the wear and tear of a children's party than those lighter in texture—are Usually accordion pleated, the yokes being worked with devices in seed pearls or scrolls of ribbon work. The sac coats, which have quite taken the place of cloaks for little girls evening wear, are being built of soft white Pyrenian wool, and delight- fully. cosy and pretty they are. Once again handkerchiefs with hemstitched coloured borders are being offered us, but the best dressed women will have none of them. The pure white varieties inset with lace, and edged with a tiny border of the same,are in our better taste, and always look dainty tucked up a sleeve or inside the coat. In veils, the latest idea is white chiffon, over which a very filmy black net is arranged. The chiffon is most efficacious in keeping off the keen cutting winds from the face. It is good for the complexion too, that white chiffon rather than black net should come in contact with the skin, and last, but by no means least, the combination is a most becoming one. The modern craze for anything old, is extending to articles of dress, and the old fob seals which men used to wear in bygone times are now salliqg for quite fancy prices, as compared with their bullion valve. There is also a keen demand for old Sheffield plate which ha.s risen many times in value during recent years, and the craze for old silver was well illustrated the other day when £3,000 was given for an Elizabethan salt cellar, or et the rate of £3OO per oz. £3,000 is not much to some people, but it is the equivalent of £l2O a year, which seems a good deal to pay for the possession of an article of purely sentimental value. There is certainly a little more ulitity in the Louis Quatorse furniture, which is also greatly prized by the hunters after anything that can claim anligruty.

WARRINGTON-NORTHWICH LIGHT RAILWAY. Concessions to Northwich. At the meeting of the Northwich Urban Council on Tuesday. the Clerk presented a report upon an interview which the Chairman and himself had had with the Light Railway Commissioners with the object of laying before them reasons why additional safeguarding clauses should be granted to Northwich in connection with the War- rington to Northwich Light Railway Order. First of all, the deputation succeeded in having a clause inserted in the order that by reason of the fact that the Northwich Council have improved Navigation-road in a manner likely to be valuable to the light railway, the company on commencing tomake the railway shall pay the Northwich Council the sum of .£l5O towards the cost of such improvement. Particular stress was laid upon some curious points connected with salt subsid- ences in the district, and the Commissioners while intimating that the clause was absolutely novel, agreed to insert in the order the fact that shourd at any time the railway be damaged by reason of the breakage of a main or sewer pipe, &c., owing to subsidence she Northwich Council should in no way be held responsible. On similar grounds they agreed that it was advisable that granite setts should be used in the con- struction of railway track, and fuather, that the Council should have power, in the event of evidence of the instability of any pole supporting wires, to secure the pole, if the company's men were not available, and to charge the cost to the railway company. It was also made clear that it was within the province of the Council to stop railway traffic pending the restoration of .a road. Some other points, such as the deposit of material during the execution of work on the railway, adequate lighting, &c., were referred to the Board of Trade. • An inquest was held at Croydon on Nelson Curtis (06), an independent gentleman, whose daughter said he never had medical attention during his life. He caught a chill, wouldn't have a doctor, and died. Death was due to heart failure and senile decay, accelerated by the weather.

THE ALLEGED SHOPBREAKING. Four Prisoners Charged at Warrington. Three arrests were made by the Warrington police last Friday in connection with the robbery from the premises of Messrs. Kay and Cowdell, Forster-street. On Saturday morning, the prisoners, whose names are George Hill, 284, linutsford-road, and William and Rachel Fogg, 1 Richardson's-row, Sankey Bridges, ap- peared before the magistrates and were charged with breaking and entering the premises of Messrs, Kay and Cowden. and stealing 14 hams, 251 b. of nailrod tobacco, 41b. of pigtail tobacco, 101 b. of bacon, a quantity of eggs and tea, and 4s. in cash. Superintendent Monaghan said that on the previout Wednesday night the shop was locked up as usual, and at twenty minutes past twelve the following morning five men were seen leaving the premises, each carrying something bulky under Ins arm. Information was given to the . police and on examining the premises it was foundthat anentrance had been effected by breaking the lock off the front door. The goods mentioned were missing. On Friday mornning Police- constable Lewis met Hill, and noticed that he was carry- ing something bulky under his arm. When asked what he was carrying he said, "Only pig iron," but when the parcel was examined it was found to contain two hams, wrapped up in a woman's skirt. The female prisoner was seen the same day by Police-constable Dyson in Sankey- street, and she was also carrying it parcel, which she said contained old clothes but this also con- tained a ham. At her house at Sankey Bridges another ham was found, and the man Fogg stated that he had bought the hams from a man in the street at Manchester. Another ham was found at the house of William Fogg's mother, and Fogg was arrested by P.S. Barnett. The prisoners were remanded until Tuesday. A MAN SURRENDERS HIMSELF BUT DENIES ROBBERY. When the case came on for hearing on Tuesday a fourth prisoner appeared in the dock. His name is John Green and he resides in Watkin-street. The value of the goods alleged to have been stolen was stated in the charge to be £ll 10s. Mr. Steel appeared for the prisoner Hill, and Mr. Browne represented the other prisoners. Superintendent Monaghan again related the facts of the case. He added that Green surrendered himself on Monday morning, and stated that he sold two barns to Hill. He found the hams near to his back door, which was close to Messrs. Kay and Cowdell's shop. He (the Superintendent) asked for a further remand for a week. Mr. Browne applied for bail on behalf of his clients. He read a letter from Mr. Walter Milner, of the White- cross Company,stating that Green had been in the firm's employ for twelve years, and was steady, sober and in- dustrious. They had no reason to doubt his honesty and his foreman, M., L. Davies, could speak as to his charac- ter as a workman. Fogg was employed by Messrs. Percival, Vicars, and Company, glass manufacturers, Manchester, and the manager for the firm wrote stating that Fogg was at work there on Wednesday night from 7 p.m. to 1 a.m. and on the following morning from 7 a.m. to 1 p.m. He (Mr. Browne) would be able to prove that Fogg was working at the time the robbery took place. Mr. Steel, in applying for bail on behalf of Hill, said his client had been in the ' employ of Messrs. Pearson and Knowles for the past eleven years and had a good character. There was absolutely nothing against him at present. He had five little .1 children and it would be very nice if he could be at 1 home at Christmas time. oguperintendent Monaghan did not object to substan- tial bail. Prisoners were remanded until Wednesday, bail being allowed n sureties of £lO each.

ASSOCIATION. LANCASHIRE LEAGUE. BAcup v. EARLESTO WK.—There was only a poor gate at Lanehead when Bacup entertained Earlestown, the recent series of defeats of the home club doubtless being responsible for this. Bacup had intended playing anew keeper from,Darwen, but, owing to non-appearance, Chase offichtted. Earlestown started minus Cottom. Bacup started, and Lee let in Boardman, who shot wide. Earlestown forced a corner, but Taylor cleared. Half- time.—Bacup 2, Earlestotm I. Final.—Bacup, 3 goals; Earlestown, 2 goals. WARRINGTON CUP. SECOND ROUND. WicIN6TON PARK V. RICHMOND ATHLETIC.—At Winnington on Saturday. With the wind in their favour in the initial half the visitors attacked, a couple of fruitless corners falling to their lot. Winnington then raced away, and Nicholls placed a splendid opening from a centre by Randles. For scone time play, ruled even, each end being visited in turn. The Parkites again attacked, and a nice Pass by Randles was turned to account by Nicholls. At half-time the score was Win- nington, one goal; Richmond, nil. In the second half another point fell to the lot of the hornesters, and the -visitors failing to respond were beaten by two goals to nil.

I Christmas Fare Missing. . lAt the Warrington Occasional Court on Monday John Jackson, Elizabeth-street. Warrington, 'was charged with stealing five turkeys, four geese and 30 Ihens, of the total value of £9 10s. the property of John 1 Taylor, Brow Farm, Sankey,thetween the' 18th and 119th December.--It was stated that on the 19th : December prisoner sold a turkey to his brother-in-law. i Information of the robbery having been given to the I police, the house of the brother-in-law was visited,and ' the turkey was found plucked and. dressed. Some of the feathers &c, were discovered on the prernises,ard these led to the identification of the turkey as the property of Mr. Taylor. Prisoner was arrested on suspicion of having stolen the other fowls,—He was remanded until Monday next. ST. HELENS V. WIGAN. The meeting of these neighbouring rivals at St. Helens attracted a large number of spectators. In the first period St. Helens had the wind in their favour, and at once took the ball to the Wigan line. After some scrummaging, Hull gained possession and dropped the leather over the Wigan bar, two minutes from the cora- mencemept. The Wigan men subsequently transferred play to the opposite end, and just missed scoring on more than one occasion. As a result of splendid work by Barr and Dickenson, the last named gained a brilli- ant try for Wigan. Just before the interval Liversage scored for St. Helens at the corner. Half-time score St. Helens, 1 goal and 1 try (5 points) ; Wigan, 1 try (3 Points.) After several attempts to score, Wigan at last got close up, and Anderson passed to Barr, who gained an easy try at the corner. During the remaining few minutes Wigan again pressed, but failed to increase their score, and Wigan won by 2 tries (6 points), to St. Helens 1 goal and 1 try (5 points). , HULL KINGSTON ROVERS V. BATLEY. Played on he Craven-street ground at Hull, before a big crowd. The Batley men made a great effort. and were decidedly dangerous, but the defence was too good, and the game ended with a blank score-sheet. LEIGH v. HULL. • This match was played at Leigh. Leigh started against the wind and slope, before nearly 3,000 specta- tors. After Frank had made an unsuccessful effort to break through, Leigh were penalised, and Jacques placed a goal. Leigh now brought out Boardman as a fifth three-quarter to stop Hull's attempt at passing, but he got off-side, and Jacques placed another, penalty goal. }full led at the interval by 4 points to nil. The score remained the same to the end,Hull winning by 2 goals to nothing. SWINTON v. HUDDERSFIELD. This match took place at Swinton before 5,000 people, and at half-time there was no score. Following up a run by Valentine, Morgan opened the scoring for Swinton by dropping a goal. Deere and W. Dewhirst played -well for Huddersfield. Tricky work by Morgan enabled him to give to Lewis at the right moment, and he scored for Swinton between the posts. Preston landed a goal. Valentine next secured a try in a good position, while before the finish Morgan also ran over, both tries resulting in Preston adding the goal points.—Result : Swinton 4 goals and 3 tries (17 points); Huddersfield nil. OLDHAM v. HALIFAX: THE LEADERS DEFEATED. Halifax suffered their second defeat of the season in their game with Oldham at Watersheddings. Arthur Lees had the principal hand in making a score possible, but S. Lees and Spottiswoode participated in the move- ment, and the last-named scored the try. With such a strong wind Halifax's chance of securing a favourable result were by no means hopeless at the restart, forthey were only five points behind, but unfortunately they had to begin again without Nettleton, who had broken one of the bones of his hand just before half-time. Bulmer came from the pack into the three-quarter line when the back division was re-arranged, but almost immediately he was also injured, and limped badly for the rest of the game. Later Joe Riley was put out of action, and alto- gether the Halifax backs had a very unlucky time Of it. On the other hand, the Oldham backs played much better than in the first half, two tries scored by Cash and Spottiswoode being remarkably well got. The goal kicks failed, and the final score was :—Oldham 1 goal and 3 tries (11 points), Halifax nil. LANCASHIRE COMBINATION. WARRINGTON "A" WIN AT ROCHDALE. Warrington "A" visited Rochdale on Saturday and defeated the Hornets "A" by two tries to one. The Hornets were the first to force the game, and Warrington had great difficulty in preventing a score. The visitors afterwards attacked vigorously, and Ed- munds failed to place a penalty goal from a fairly easy position. Warrington passed smartly, and scored at the corner, but no goal resulted. Warrington showed splen- did combination, and Edmunds scored after a fine run. Half time score: Warrington, two tries (six points); Rochdale Hornets, nil. The Hornets started the second half in fine fashion, and after sharp passing Grindrod scored. Beckwith failed at goal. Final: Warrington 'A," two tries; Rochdale Hornets "A," one try. FRIENDLY MATCH. RUNCORN FEREILIZERS v. WIDNES RANGERS.—FIayed at Halton. Gallagher, of the Rangers, kicked off against the wind. A few minutes after the start, Woods intercepted a pass from Kennedy and scored a try, the same player kicking a goal. The Rangers' forwards screwed the pack. and Dale picked the ball up from a dribble and scored, no goal resulting. Half-time score: 'Freeilizers, 5 points; Rangers, 3 points. After some scrambling play near the visitors' line Watson picked up and scored, no goal resulting. Final : Fereilizers, 1 goal 2 sries (8 points; Rangers, 1 try (3 points). A Lovett scout named William Morris was sentenced to eight years' imprisonment, at Glasgow on Tuesday, for shooting at his wife with a revolver. A letter was read from Lord Lovett, in which-he described the c,nn- duct of the prisoner in South Africa as heroic. "You are no more fit to look after children than I am to look after a motor car," said the Salford Coroner on Tuesday to a mother whose two-years-old child had been fed on bread and cheese and beer" because it asked for it." COMMERCIAL FamMEs.—According to Kemp's , Mercantile Gazette," the number of New Bankruptcy Proceedings in England and Wales gazetted during the week ending December 20th, was 77. The number in the corresponding week of last year was 90, showing a decrease of 13, being a net decrease in 1902, to date,of 26. William Bolton, marine engineer, was executed on , Tuesday morning at Hull for the murder of Jane Allen. After stabbing the woman, Bolton (who was separated ; from his wife) attempted suicide by cutting his throat. ' It is supposedhe became jealous when Allen told him she was getting married. At the trial the defence was that ; Bolton stabbed the woman while he was in a semi- conscious condition after sleep. After his condemna- tion Bolton was visited by friends and relatives, and seemed cheerful. He made no reference to his crime. His interview with his children was very affecting, the murderer showing extreme distress. Bolton gave no trouble to the prison officials, and maintained a fairly good appetite to the end.
